Symbolic Automata: ω \omega -Regularity Modulo Theories
Margus Veanes, Thomas Ball, Gabriel Ebner, Olli Saarikivi

Abstract
. Symbolic automata are finite state automata that support potentially infinite alphabets, such as the set of rational numbers, generally applied to regular expressions/languages over finite words. In symbolic automata (or automata modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} ), an alphabet is represented by an effective Boolean algebra 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , supported by a decision procedure for satisfiability. Regular languages over infinite words (so called ω \omega -regular languages) have a rich history paralleling that of regular languages over finite words, with well known applications to model checking via Büchi automata and temporal logics. We generalize symbolic automata to support ω \omega -regular languages via symbolic transition terms and symbolic derivatives , bringing together a variety of classic automata and logics in a unified framework that provides all the necessary ingredients to support symbolic model checking modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} . In particular, we define: (1) alternating Büchi automata modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} as well (non-alternating) non-deterministic Büchi automata modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} ( NBW 𝒜 \textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}} ); (2) an alternation elimination algorithm that incrementally constructs an NBW 𝒜 \textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}} from an ABW 𝒜 \textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}} , and can also be used for constructing the product of two NBW 𝒜 \textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}} ; (3) a definition of linear temporal logic (LTL) modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} that generalizes Vardi’s construction of alternating Büchi automata from LTL, using (2) to go from LTL modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} to NBW 𝒜 \textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}} via ABW 𝒜 \textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}} . Finally, we present a combination of LTL modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} with extended regular expressions modulo 𝒜 \mathcal{A} that generalizes the Property Specification Language (PSL). Our combination allows regex complement , that is not supported in PSL but can be supported naturally by using symbolic transition terms.



1.
Introduction

Classical finite automata, which correspond to regular expressions/languages,
are finite in
three orthogonal dimensions
:

•

the number of
states
in the automata, which make it a
finite
(or
finite-state
) automata;

•

the number of
alphabet elements
that induce state transitions;

•

the length of the
words
(a sequence of alphabet elements) recognized by the automata.

Symbolic automata
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2021
)
are finite state automata that support potentially infinite alphabets,
such as the set of rational numbers. The transitions of symbolic automata are labeled with predicates
from an effective Boolean algebra
\mathcal{A}
. Each atomic predicate and their Boolean combination in
\mathcal{A}

can compactly represent a (possibly infinite) subset of elements.

The power of symbolic automata
is to make use of decision procedures for
\mathcal{A}

(
de Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
to avoid the
need for reduction to the propositional (classic) case, which can incur an exponential blowup.
For example, a propositional encoding of a Boolean expression of size
N
in
\mathcal{A}
would
require
O(2^{N})
conjunctions of atomic predicates in a propositional setting (so called
“mintermization”). In some cases, a classical algorithm for finite
automata can be lifted straightforwardly to symbolic automata; in other cases,
entirely new algorithms are required
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2021
)
.

Regular languages over infinite words (so called
\omega
-regular languages)
have a rich history paralleling that of regular languages over finite words;
In fact, the two are intimately connected:
(
McNaughton 1966
)

showed that
\omega
-regular languages are equivalently described
by Büchi automata and the union of a finite set of
\omega
-regular expressions,
where each expression has the form
{\alpha_{i}}^{*}{\beta_{i}}^{\omega}
,
where
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
are regular expressions for finite words
and
\beta_{i}
is infinitely repeated using the
\omega
operator.
Alternating Büchi automata have equivalent expressive power as Büchi automata,
but can be exponentially more compact
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
.

Furthermore,
\omega
-regular languages are an important part of the theoretical foundation
for model checking of reactive systems
(
Clarke et al. 1999
)
, where both the system and (many of) the
properties to be verified of the system are described by sets of infinite traces. Various
forms of automata, including Büchi automata, can be used to describe the system while
temporal logics describe the properties to be checked. In particular,
Linear temporal logic (LTL)
(
Pnueli 1977
)
is used to describe the
expected behavior of reactive systems and
can be lowered to Büchi automata via a variety of algorithms.
As LTL does not fully capture
\omega
-regular languages,
various extensions of LTL incorporate regular expressions
(
Eisner and Fisman 2006
;
Duret-Lutz 2022
)

to increase its expressive capability. The LTL-based automata-theoretic approach to model checking
constructs the product of the system automata with the Büchi automata representing
the negation of the LTL formula and then analyzes whether the resulting automata has any
accepting runs
(
Vardi and Wolper 1986
)
. If there are no accepting runs then the system automata
is verified with respect to the LTL formula.

1.1.
Symbolic transition terms and symbolic derivatives modulo
\mathcal{A}

We generalize symbolic automata to
\omega
-regular
languages by first lifting the concept of
transition regexes

from
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
to
symbolic transition terms
, which are parameteric over two domains:

•

alphabet
, with an effective Boolean algebra
\mathcal{A}
over it;

•

leaves
, which represent the language of the automata, with its own effective Boolean algebra

Symbolic transition terms can be used as the basis of defining the semantics of languages/logics and their corresponding
automata using
symbolic derivatives
, providing a unifying framework for automated reasoning and rewriting that operates
incrementally over a symbolic representation (Section
3
).

In our formulation,
\varrho(\phi)
is a

symbolic derivative modulo
\mathcal{A}
that works as a

curried
form of a classical transition function
\rho(\phi,a)
,
where
a
is a concrete alphabet element. Note that the decision of actually
evaluating
\rho(\phi,a)
is avoided in
\varrho(\phi)
(each predicate in
\mathcal{A}
represents
a potentially infinite set of alphabet elements).

An example of an LTL formula modulo an SMT solver is

\varphi\land\psi
with
\varphi=\mathbf{G}(0\,{<}\,x)
and

\psi=(2\,{\shortmid}\,x)\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}(3\,{\shortmid}\,x)
where
x
is a variable of
type integer. The formula states that
x
is always positive and
remains even until divisible by
3
. Then, for example,

(x{\mapsto}2)(x{\mapsto}4)(x{\mapsto}3)(x{\mapsto}1)^{\omega}\models\varphi
.
Here
(x{\mapsto}n)
is a member of the
infinite alphabet

consisting of all possible SMT interpretations. The symbolic
derivative of
\varphi\land\psi
is the following nested
if-then-else (ITE) expression, or
transition term
,

\varrho(\varphi\land\psi)=((0\,{<}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,((3\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,((2\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\varphi\land\psi)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot))\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)

whose
conditions
are the SMT predicates
(0\,{<}\,x)
,
(2\,{\shortmid}\,x)
, and
(3\,{\shortmid}\,x)
,
and whose
leaves
are the LTL formulas
\varphi
,
\varphi\land\psi
, and
\bot
(
false
).
E.g.,
{\varrho(\varphi\land\psi)}{(x{\mapsto}8)}=\varphi\land\psi
because

(0\,{<}\,8)
,

(3\,{\nshortmid}\,8)
, and

(2\,{\shortmid}\,8)
.

A very useful
duality
principle of symbolic derivatives
is that
\varrho(\lnot\phi)=\lnot\varrho(\phi)
. So, by using the
standard negation law of ITEs

\lnot(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\lnot f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\lnot g)
, it follows
in this particular case that

\displaystyle\varrho(\lnot(\varphi\land\psi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lnot(((0\,{<}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,((3\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,((2\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\varphi\land\psi)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot))\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((0\,{<}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,((3\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\lnot\varphi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,((2\,{\shortmid}\,x)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\lnot(\varphi\land\psi)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\top))\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\top)

The same principle avoids the need for
determinization
of
symbolic finite automata

in the context of
extended regular expression (regex) constraint solving
,
by
lazily propagating
complement of regex
derivatives in Z3
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
. It also avoids
symbolic
alternating finite automata
(s-AFA) normalization

(
D’Antoni et al. 2018
)
needed
for complement, whose transitions are
represented in flat form by
{q}\,{\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scriptsize$\xrightarrow{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\alpha\\[-3.27773pt]
\end{array}}$}}}\,{\phi}
where
q
is a
state,
\alpha
is a predicate of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\phi
is a positive
Boolean combination of states. Currently the only known technique to normalize an s-AFA amounts
to computing all the satisfiable Boolean combinations of the guards that is
exponential time in the worst case.

1.2.
Contributions and Overview

We show how symbolic transition terms and derivatives can be used to generalize symbolic
automata to work with
\omega
-regular languages modulo an infinite alphabet represented by
\mathcal{A}
,
bringing together a variety of classic automata and logics in a unified framework. In particular, we give:

•

a definition of alternating Büchi automata modulo
\mathcal{A}

(
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
) and their relationship to classical alternating Büchi automata as well as to (non-alternating) non-deterministic Büchi automata modulo
\mathcal{A}
(
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
) and deterministic Büchi automata modulo
\mathcal{A}
(
\textit{DBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
) (Section
4
)

•

an alternation elimination algorithm, a symbolic generalization
of
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
that incrementally
constructs an
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
from an
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
(Section
5
)

•

a definition of linear temporal logic (LTL)
(
Pnueli 1977
)
modulo
\mathcal{A}
that generalizes Vardi’s
construction of alternating Büchi automata from LTL
(
Vardi 1995
)
– with the previous results, this gives a way to go from LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
to
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
(Section
6
);

Finally, we bring the above results together to demonstrate a
combination of LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
with extended regular expressions modulo
\mathcal{A}

(
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
) that generalizes SPOT
(
Duret-Lutz 2022
)
and PSL
(
Eisner and Fisman 2006
)
(Section
7
).
Our combination allows regex
complement
, that is not supported in SPOT/PSL
but can be supported naturally by using transition terms.
We also lift the classical concept of
\omega
-regular
languages
(
Büchi 1960
;
McNaughton 1966
)
as the languages accepted by
ABW
so as to
be modulo
\mathcal{A}
, and show that
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
precisely captures
\omega
-regularity modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

Section
8
reviews related work
and Section
9
discusses future work.
Section
10
concludes the paper.
The appendix has proofs for the theorems and lemmas
whose proofs were not included in the main part of the paper.

2.
Preliminaries

As a meta-notation throughout the paper
we write
\textit{lhs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textit{rhs}

to let
lhs
be
equal by definition
to
rhs
.
In the following let
\mathbb{D}
be a nonempty (possibly infinite) domain of
core elements.
The natural numbers are denoted by
\mathbb{N}
.

2.1.
Effective Boolean Algebras and Boolean Closures

An
effective Boolean algebra over
\mathbb{D}

is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{D},\Psi,\llbracket\_\rrbracket,\bot,\top,\lor,\land,\neg)

where
\Psi
is a set of

predicates
closed under the Boolean connectives;
\llbracket\_\rrbracket:\Psi\rightarrow 2^{\mathbb{D}}
is a
denotation function
;
\bot,\top\in\Psi
;
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket=\emptyset
,
\llbracket\top\rrbracket=\mathbb{D}
, and for all

\alpha,\beta\in\Psi
,
\llbracket\alpha\lor\beta\rrbracket=\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\alpha\land\beta\rrbracket=\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
, and
\llbracket\neg\alpha\rrbracket=\mathbb{D}\setminus\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket
.
We also write
a\vDash\alpha
for
a\in\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket
.
For
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi
we
write
\alpha\equiv\beta
to mean
\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket=\llbracket\beta\rrbracket
. In
particular, if
\alpha\equiv\bot
then
\alpha
is

unsatisfiable
, also denoted by
\textit{UNSAT}(\alpha)
.
We require that the connectives are computable
and that satisfiability is decidable in
\mathcal{A}
.
We use
\mathcal{A}
as a subscript to indicate a component of
\mathcal{A}
, e.g.,

\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
is the set of predicates of
\mathcal{A}
. We omit the
subscript when it follows from the context.

Let
\Gamma\subseteq\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
be finite. A
minterm
of
\Gamma
is
a
satisfiable
predicate
(\bigwedge\!S)\land\lnot\!\bigvee(\Gamma{\setminus}S)

for some
S\subseteq\Gamma
.
\textit{Minterms}(\Gamma)
, say
\Sigma
,
denotes the set of all minterms of
\Gamma
. The
core
properties
of
\Sigma
are that all minterms are satisfiable and
mutually disjoint, and that each satisfiable predicate in
\Gamma
is
equivalent to a disjunction of some minterms. Thus,
\Sigma
defines
a
finite partition
of
\mathbb{D}
. For example, if

\Gamma=\{\alpha,\beta\}
then
\Sigma
is the set of all satisfiable
predicates in

\{\alpha\land\beta,\lnot\alpha\land\beta,\alpha\land\lnot\beta,\lnot\alpha\land\lnot\beta\}
.

We let
O^{\textrm{sat}}_{\mathcal{A}}(n)
denote the computational complexity of checking
satisfiability in
\mathcal{A}
for predicates
\psi
of size
|\psi|=n
.
Here we make the standard assumption that the size of a predicate is
the sum of the sizes of its immediate subexpressions. Under this assumption it
follows that the complexity of computing
\Sigma
is

O(2^{{O^{\textrm{sat}}_{\mathcal{A}}(n)}})
where
n=\sum_{i<k}|\gamma_{i}|
and

\Gamma=\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i<k}
. Observe also that
|\Sigma|\leq 2^{k}
, i.e.,
the
number
of minterms of
\Gamma
is in the worst-case exponential in
k
.

Boolean Closure

Given a set
S
,
we define the Boolean closure
\mathbb{B}(S)
of
S
such that
S\cup\{\bot,\top\}\subseteq\mathbb{B}(S)
and

\mathbb{B}(S)
is closed under
\lor
,
\land
, and
\lnot
, where

\bot
and
\top
act as
the corresponding unit and zero of the Boolean
operations, satisfying
\bot\land s=\bot
,
\top\land s=s
,

\top\lor s=\top
,
\bot\lor s=s
,
\neg\bot=\top
, and
\neg\top=\bot
.
1
1

1

S
may already include
\bot
and
\top
and it may also be that
S=\mathbb{B}(S)
.

We write
\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(S)
for the subset of
\mathbb{B}(S)

where
\lnot
is omitted.

The disjunctive normal form of
\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(S)

is a disjunction
\bigvee_{i=0}^{n}\psi_{i}
where each
\psi_{i}
is a conjunction
\bigwedge X_{i}

for some
X_{i}\subseteq S
.

Formally
, we adopt the
set-of-sets
representation
of
\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)
as
\{X_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
For example,
\mathrm{DNF}((s_{1}\lor s_{2})\land s_{3})=\{\{s_{1},s_{3}\},\{s_{2},s_{3}\}\}

that stands for a formula
(s_{1}\land s_{3})\lor(s_{2}\land s_{3})
.
Observe, as a special case, in
DNF,
\emptyset
denotes
\bot
and
\{\emptyset\}
denotes

\top
.
One advantage of this representation is that then
commutativity, associativity and idempotence of the
intended semantics
of the Boolean operations
is built-in to
\mathrm{DNF}(\psi)
.

2.2.
Infinite/Finite Sequences and Derivatives

An
infinite word

w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is a function from
\mathbb{N}
to

\mathbb{D}
. We let
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}w(i)
denote the
i
’th
element of
w
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
a\in\mathbb{D}
and
v\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
then
u=a{\cdot}v
denotes the
sequence such that if
i=0
then
u_{i}=a
else
u_{i}=v_{i-1}
. If

S\subseteq\mathbb{D}
and
L\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
then

S{\cdot}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{a{\cdot}v\mid a\in S,v\in L\}
. If

v\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
then
v_{(n..)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\lambda i.v_{n+i}
denotes
the suffix of
v
starting with
v_{n}
. If
a\in\mathbb{D}
then
a^{\omega}\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
such that

a^{\omega}_{i}=a
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Concatenation (
\cdot
) binds stronger than
intersection (
\cap
) that binds stronger than union (
\cup
).
We use juxtaposition for
concatenation when this is unambiguous.

For
finite words

v\in\mathbb{D}^{*}

we share the same notation as for infinite words that
v_{i}
is the

i
’th element of
v
and
v_{(i..)}
is the
i
’th rest of
v
, when

0\leq i<|v|
with
|v|
as the length of
v
.
We also let
v_{(|v|..)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\epsilon
.
Note that
v_{(0..)}=v
.
We also define
x\prec y
to mean that

x\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
is a nonempty proper prefix of
y
where
y\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
or
y\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
.
We also let
x\prec_{\epsilon}y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}x\prec y\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(x=\epsilon\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}y\neq\epsilon)
.
We write
vw
for the concatenation of
v
with
w
where

w\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
or
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
and lift this definition to sets, as ususal.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be either
\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
or
\mathbb{D}^{*}
, with the
associated definition of complement
\complement\!\left(L\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{D}\setminus L
for
L\subseteq\mathfrak{D}
.
The
derivative of
L
for
a\in\mathbb{D}
is

\mathbf{D}_{a}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{v\mid av\in L\}
.
These facts hold for all
a\in\mathbb{D},L,R\subseteq\mathfrak{D}
:

\mathbf{D}_{a}(\complement\!\left(L\right))=\complement\!\left(\mathbf{D}_{a}(L)\right),\quad\mathbf{D}_{a}(L\cup R)=\mathbf{D}_{a}(L)\cup\mathbf{D}_{a}(R),\quad\mathbf{D}_{a}(L\cap R)=\mathbf{D}_{a}(L)\cap\mathbf{D}_{a}(R).

As a convention throughout the paper when a term
t
is associated
with a language over
\mathbb{D}^{*}
we denote that by
\mathcal{L}(t)

and when
t
is associated with a language
over
\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
we denote that by
\mathscr{L}(t)
.

3.
Transition Terms

As shown in the introduction,
transition terms
are the target language that
represents the derivative-based compositional semantics of the source language (such as
regular expressions, LTL, as well as their combination in RLTL);
they generalize the notion of transition regexes from
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{D},\Psi,\llbracket\_\rrbracket,\bot,\top,\mathrel{|},\mathrel{\&},{\mathrm{!}})
be a given effective Boolean algebra
as the given
element algebra
and let
\Phi
be a generic type
of
leaves
that in general is independent of
\mathcal{A}
.

Transition terms

{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Phi\rangle}

(
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}
or
{\mathcal{T}\,}
for short when both parameters are clear) are defined as
the following nested if-then-else expressions, i.e., ordered labelled binary
trees whose internal nodes have labels in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and leaves belong to
\Phi
.
We let
\phi
range over
\Phi
and
\alpha
range over
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
.

•

if
\phi\in\Phi
then
\phi\in{\mathcal{T}\,}
,
\phi
is called a
leaf
;

•

if
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
f,g\in{\mathcal{T}\,}
then
(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)\in{\mathcal{T}\,}
is called an

ITE
and
\alpha
is called its
condition
.

Given
a\in\mathbb{D}
and
f\in{\mathcal{T}\,}
, the
leaf of

f
for
a
, denoted by
f{\downarrow}a
, is defined as follows.

\phi{\downarrow}a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\phi,\qquad(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g){\downarrow}a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}f{\downarrow}a,&\textrm{{if} $a\in\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket$;}\\
g{\downarrow}a,&\textrm{{otherwise}.}\end{array}\right.

The set of all leaves and conditions of a transition term are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\quad}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\textit{Leaves}(\phi)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\{\phi\}&\textit{Leaves}((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\textit{Leaves}(f)\cup\textit{Leaves}(g)\\
\textit{Conds}(\phi)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\emptyset&\textit{Conds}((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\{\alpha\}\cup\textit{Conds}(f)\cup\textit{Conds}(g)\end{array}

Any binary operation
\diamond:\Phi\times\Phi\rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}
,
is lifted to
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}\times{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi^{\prime}\rangle}
as
follows:
2
2

2

The choice of rule in the case when
h
is an ITE can be
based on a total ordering over conditions (
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
) to maintain uniformity.

(1)

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\qquad}rcl}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)\diamond h&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\diamond h\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g\diamond h)&h\diamond(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,h\diamond f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,h\diamond g)\end{array}

Any unary operation
\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}:\Phi\rightarrow\Phi^{\prime}
is lifted
to
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi^{\prime}\rangle}
analogously as follows:

(2)

\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}g)

The following generic properties of transition terms are used frequently
in many constructions where
\diamond
and
\blacklozenge
are lifted binary and unary operations.

Lemma 1.

Let
f,g\in{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Phi\rangle}
and
a\in\mathbb{D}
. Then

(f\diamond g){\downarrow}a=f{\downarrow}a\diamond g{\downarrow}a

and
(\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}f){\downarrow}a=\textrm{\scriptsize$\blacklozenge$}(f{\downarrow}a)
.

We say that two transition terms
f
and
g

are
functionally equivalent
, that we denote by
f\cong g
, when

\forall a\in\mathbb{D}:f{\downarrow}a=g{\downarrow}a
.
We use
condition elimination

(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,f)\cong f
and

flattening

(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)\cong(\alpha\mathrel{\&}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)

(analogously for the symmetrical cases), as particular generic simplifications.

3.1.
Cleaning Modulo
\mathcal{A}

The internal branching structure of nested ITEs is irrelevant as long
as the leaf values remain the same for any given element
a\in\mathbb{D}
.
All simplification rules that preserve functional equivalence are
allowed. The most critical rule, that we call
cleaning
here,
eliminates dead branches and involves the use of Boolean operations
and
satisfiability modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

A transition term
f
is called
clean
when
all of its subterms are reachable, i.e., when
f
has
no
infeasible paths
. The
restriction of
f
to

\beta\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
is denoted here by
f{\restriction}\beta

and
cleaning of
f
means to rewrite
f
to
f{\restriction}\top
, i.e.,

f\cong f{\restriction}\top
.

\phi{\restriction}\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\phi\qquad(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g){\restriction}\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g{\restriction}\beta,&\textrm{if}\;\textit{UNSAT}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\alpha);\\
f{\restriction}\beta,&\textrm{else if}\;\textit{UNSAT}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha);\\
(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f{\restriction}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\alpha)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g{\restriction}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)),&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

For example,
{(\top\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\_)}\cong f
. All binary operations
(
1
) can be assumed to produce a clean result (in most uses)
in an implementation, that can take into account that the arguments
are clean to begin with, when this is known, and also use caching to
avoid redundant cleaning operations.

Example 3.1.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
be two predicates such that

\emptyset\neq\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\subsetneq\llbracket\beta\rrbracket\neq\mathbb{D}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}((\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{1}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{2})\land(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{4})){\restriction}\top&=&(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{1}\land(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{4})\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{2}\land(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{4})){\restriction}\top\\
&=&(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{1}\land\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{1}\land\__{4})\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{2}\land\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{2}\land\__{4})){\restriction}\top\\
&=&(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{1}\land\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{1}\land\__{4}){\restriction}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\__{2}\land\__{3}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\__{2}\land\__{4}){\restriction}{\mathrm{!}}\beta)\\
&=&(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\__{1}\land\__{3}){\restriction}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\alpha)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\__{1}\land\__{4}){\restriction}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha))\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\__{2}\land\__{4}){\restriction}{\mathrm{!}}\beta)\end{array}

where cleaning eliminated the infeasible path to
\__{2}\land\__{3}

because
{\mathrm{!}}\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\alpha
is unsatisfiable.

\Box

3.2.
Boolean Operations and INF Normal Form

We will be considering
\Phi=\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(S)
where
S
is some set
whose members are called
states
of an automaton,
or
S
is a set of formulas and
S=\mathbb{B}(S)
.
In either case (
1
) and (
2
) are
used to lift the Boolean operations to
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}
.

When we work with
\phi\in\Phi=\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(S)
it is useful to work instead with
the canonical representation
\boldsymbol{\phi}=\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)
. Recall, that

\boldsymbol{\phi}\subseteq 2^{S}
represents the formula

\bigvee_{X\in\boldsymbol{\phi}}\bigwedge X
in
\Phi
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\phi}=\emptyset
represents
\bot
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}=\{\emptyset\}
represents

\top
(the empty conjunction is, by definition,
\top
, in
\bigvee_{X\in\{\emptyset\}}\bigwedge X
).

We say that
f\in{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathrm{DNF}(\Phi)\rangle}
is the
ITE-DNF
or

INF normal form
of the corresponding
term in
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}
. The following definitions of

\land
and
\lor
preserve DNF, and are lifted to
{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathrm{DNF}(\Phi)\rangle}
by (
1
).
Let
\varphi,\psi\in\Phi
,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\mathrm{DNF}(\varphi)
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}=\mathrm{DNF}(\psi)
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\lor\boldsymbol{\psi}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cup\boldsymbol{\psi}=\mathrm{DNF}(\varphi\lor\psi)\\
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\land\boldsymbol{\psi}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\{X\cup Y\mid X\in\boldsymbol{\varphi},Y\in\boldsymbol{\psi}\}=\mathrm{DNF}(\varphi\land\psi)\end{array}

One advantage of INF is that if
S
is
finite
then so is

\mathrm{DNF}(\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(S))
.
A potential disadvantage is the worst-case exponential complexity of
computing DNF from a given formula. However, in practice INF will be
applied both
lazily
(on a “need to know” basis) and
locally
to individual leaves
that tend to be small, rather than globally to large formulas.

When working with
f
and
g
in
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}
we lift their functional equivalence to hold modulo INF:

f\cong g\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{D}:\mathit{INF}(f){\downarrow}a=\mathit{INF}(g){\downarrow}a

In particular observe that
\bot\land f\cong\bot
,
\top\land f\cong f
,

\bot\lor f\cong f
, and
\bot\land f\cong\bot
.
We use the convention that when we write
\textit{lhs}\cong\textit{rhs}
then
it implicitly means that

rhs
is a
simplification
of
lhs
.
Observe also that
\mathit{INF}(f){\downarrow}a=\mathrm{DNF}(f{\downarrow}a)
.

We frequently use the abbreviation
(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)

as well as the following functional equivalences in
{\mathcal{T}\langle\Phi\rangle}
:

(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f){\land}(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,g)\cong(\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f{\land}g)
and

(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)\lor(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)\cong(\alpha{\mathrel{|}}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)
.

4.
Alternating Büchi Automata Modulo
\mathcal{A}

We now turn our attention to
alternating Büchi automata (which admit a linear time
translation from LTL
(
Tsay and Vardi 2021
)
) and show how they can be generalized
using transition terms so as to be modulo

\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{D},\Psi,\llbracket\_\rrbracket,\bot,\top,\mathrel{|},\mathrel{\&},{\mathrm{!}})
.
The key aspect of the following definition is that transitions
in the automata are represented symbolically by
transition terms in
{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)\rangle}
.

An
alternating Büchi automaton modulo
\mathcal{A}
(
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
) is
a tuple
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
where
Q
is a
finite

set of
states
,
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
is an
initial state combination
,

F\subseteq Q
is a set of
accepting states
, and

\varrho:Q\rightarrow{{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)\rangle}}
is a
transition function

such that if
\top
occurs in
\varrho
then

\top\in F
and
\varrho(\top)=\top
, and if

\bot
occurs in
\varrho
then

\bot\in Q{\setminus}F
and
\varrho(\bot)=\bot
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-18.00005pt]
\hskip-85.35826pt\hbox to154.05pt{\vbox to45.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.09973pt\lower-23.13428pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\end{array}

Figure 1
.
ABW
for
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)
.

\mathcal{M}
is
nondeterministic
or an
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
if

\land
occurs neither in
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}
nor in
\varrho
;
\mathcal{M}

is
deterministic
or a
\textit{DBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
if
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}\in Q
and
neither
\land
nor
\lor
occur
in
\varrho
. We indicate a component of
\mathcal{M}
by using
\mathcal{M}
as the
subscript, e.g.,
\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}
, unless
\mathcal{M}
is clear from the context.

In figures we show each transition term
\varrho(q)
by
symbolic
transitions

{q}\,{\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scriptsize$\xrightarrow{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\beta\\[-3.27773pt]
\end{array}}$}}}\,{\phi}
where
\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
is a leaf of
\varrho(q)

guarded
by the accumulated branch condition
\beta\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
from the root
of
\varrho(q)
to
\phi
.
3
3

3

If there are multiple occurrences of

\phi
in
\varrho(q)
then
\beta
is the disjunction in
\mathcal{A}
of the
corresponding branch conditions. We typically omit transitions to
\bot

and transitions into disjunctions of states
in an
NBW
are shown as separate transitions.

For example, the
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
in
Figure
1
has
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}=q_{0}
and

\varrho=\{q_{0}{\mapsto}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,q_{2}{\land}q_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{1}{\land}q_{0}),\;q_{1}{\mapsto}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{1}),\;q_{2}{\mapsto}(!\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{2}),\;\top{\mapsto}\top\}

Boolean operations
\diamond\in\{\land,\lor\}
over
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
such that
Q_{\mathcal{M}}\cap Q_{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\{\bot,\top\}
,
are defined by:

\mathcal{M}\diamond\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{A},\;Q_{\mathcal{M}}\cup Q_{\mathcal{N}},\;Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!\mathcal{M}\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}\diamond Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!\mathcal{N}\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\;\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}\cup\varrho_{\mathcal{N}},\;F_{\mathcal{M}}\cup F_{\mathcal{N}})

We say that
\mathcal{M}
(or
\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}
) is
clean
when all
the transition terms in
\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}
are clean. For example,
the
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
in Figure
1
is clean provided that
both
\alpha
and
{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
are satisfiable in
\mathcal{A}
,
i.e.,
\alpha\nequiv\bot\nequiv{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
.

4.1.
Runs and Languages

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
be an
ABW
.
The standard defintion of runs uses
X\subseteq Q

to specify
satisfying assignments
for
\varphi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
.
4
4

4

X\subseteq Q

satisfies

\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
iff the valuation

\{q\mapsto\mathbf{true}\mid q\in X\}\cup\{q\mapsto\mathbf{false}\mid q\in Q\setminus X\}
satisfies
\phi

(
Vardi 1995
)
.

Here we make use of the
fact that
X
satisifies
\varphi
iff some
X_{\min}\subseteq X
satisfies
\varphi

in a minimal manner

(
Kupferman 2018
, 5.1)
, defined by
X_{\min}
being an element of:
5
5

5

Recall that
\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)\subseteq 2^{Q}
where
\mathrm{DNF}(\bot)=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{DNF}(\top)=\{\emptyset\}
.

(3)

\{Z\mid Z\in\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}\nexists Y\in\mathrm{DNF}(\phi):Y\subsetneq Z\}

Since
(\ref{eq:MinSat})\subseteq\mathrm{DNF}(\varphi)
,
it suffices below to limit the satisfying assignments
X
of
\varphi

to
X\in\mathrm{DNF}(\varphi)
.

Let
aw\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}

and
q\in Q
. A
run for
aw
from

q
is a (potentially
infinite
)
Q
-labelled tree
\tau
whose root node has label

q
and there exists
X\in\mathrm{DNF}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)
such that,
for each
p\in X
,
there is an immediate subtree
\tau_{p}
of
\tau
such that
\tau_{p}
is a run for
w
from
p
.

A run
\tau
is
accepting
if
all
of
the infinite branches of
\tau
visit
F
infinitely often,
where an
infinite branch
visits the root of
\tau
and then continues
as an infinite branch from some immediate subtree of
\tau
.
For
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
and
q\in Q
:
w
is accepted from
q
iff there exists an
accepting run for
w
from
q
; the
language of
q
in
\mathcal{M}
is
defined and lifted to
\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
as follows:

(4)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}\mid\textrm{$w$ is accepted from $q$}\}\\
\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{q}{\lor}\mathbf{p})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{q})\cup\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{p})\quad\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{q}{\land}\mathbf{p})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{q})\cap\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{p})\quad\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}})\end{array}

It follows that

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}\land\mathcal{N})=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})\cap\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{N})
and

\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}\lor\mathcal{N})=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})\cup\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{N})
, corresponding to the
classical case
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 20)
.

Let the
INF normal form
of
\varrho
be

\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathit{INF}(\varrho(q))
for all
q\in Q
. Thus,
\boldsymbol{\varrho}
is
semantically equivalent to
\varrho
and it follows from the
definition of INF that

\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q){\downarrow}a=\mathrm{DNF}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)
. E.g., in the

\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
in Figure
1
,

\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\{\{q_{2},q_{0}\}\}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\{\{q_{1},q_{0}\}\})
.

The notion of a language of
\mathcal{M}

relative
to a state
q\in Q_{\mathcal{M}}
is
typically left implicit classically.
Recall that a classical
ABW
is a tuple
M=(\Sigma,Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\rho,F)

where
\rho:Q\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
where we assume,
w.l.o.g, that
\rho
is
total
for
\Sigma
and
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
.
For

q\in Q
, let
M^{(q)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\Sigma,Q,q,\rho,F)
where
q
is
thus the
acting initial state
. We use the definition

\mathscr{L}_{M}(q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}(M^{(q)})
below.

4.2.
From
ABW
Modulo
\mathcal{A}
to Classical
ABW

A variety of decidability results for
ABW
modulo
\mathcal{A}

follow directly from the fact that any
\mathcal{M}
, as defined above,
can be reduced to a classical
ABW
(this does not imply a practical
implementation scheme, however, as it requires mintermization).

Let
\Sigma=\textit{Minterms}(\Gamma)
where

\Gamma=\textit{Conds}(\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textit{Conds}(\varrho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{q\in Q}\textit{Conds}(\varrho(q))
,
and for
a\in\mathbb{D}
let
\widehat{a}\in\Sigma

denote the minterm such that
a\in\llbracket\widehat{a}\rrbracket
. It follows that

\forall a,b\in\mathbb{D},q\in Q:\widehat{a}=\widehat{b}\Rightarrow\varrho(q){\downarrow}a=\varrho(q){\downarrow}b

from minterms because for any ITE

(\gamma\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)
in
\varrho
we have that
\gamma\in\Gamma
, and thus,

\llbracket\widehat{a}\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket\neq\emptyset\Leftrightarrow\llbracket\widehat{a}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
.
Moreover, this holds
for all
Boolean combinations
\gamma
over
\Gamma
, i.e., individual elements of
a minterm are indistinguishable in
\mathcal{M}
.

Thus, the
classical transition function

\widehat{\varrho}(q,\widehat{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\varrho(q){\downarrow}a

for
q\in Q
and
\widehat{a}\in\Sigma

is well-defined in the
ABW

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\Sigma,Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\widehat{\varrho},F)
. We lift

\widehat{a}\in\Sigma
to
\widehat{w}\in\Sigma^{\omega}
.

Lemma 2 (Minterm).

\forall\mathcal{M}\in\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}},\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q_{\mathcal{M}}),w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}:\widehat{w}\in\mathscr{L}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}(\phi)\Leftrightarrow w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi).

Lemma
2
is a useful tool in lifting results from
the classical setting. In
particular,
decidability of nonemptiness
of
\mathcal{M}
, provided
that
\mathcal{A}
is effective, follows then directly from decidability of
nonemptiness of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, by reduction to
NBW

(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
and
decidability of nonemptiness of
NBW

(
Rabin 1970
)
.
The cost of computing
\Sigma

can be exponential in
|\mathcal{M}|
(the size of
\mathcal{M}
) that is essentially
determined by the size of
\varrho
, observe also that
|\Sigma|\leq 2^{|\Gamma|}
, and may involve exponentially many Boolean operations
and satisfiability checks in
\mathcal{A}
.

4.3.
Nondeterministic and Clean Case

Decidability of nonemptiness of a clean
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
has
linear
time
complexity, that follows by adapting the corresponding result
from
(
Emerson and Lei 1985
;
Emerson and Lei 1987
)
, where cleaning, as a separate preprocessing
step, has time complexity
O^{\textrm{sat}}_{\mathcal{A}}(|\mathcal{M}|)
. In particular, we
implemented the

nested depth-first cycle detection algorithm

(
Courcoubetis et al. 1992
, Algorithm B)

without any real changes for nonemptiness checking of a clean

\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Many other decision problems, such as
incremental
dead-state detection

(
Stanford and Veanes 2023
)
, can be used similarly. The up-front
worst-case exponential cost of converting
\mathcal{M}
to a classical
ABW

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}

is often avoidable in the symbolic setting by lifting of the
classical algorithms to work modulo
\mathcal{A}
. Similar observations
hold in the case of automata modulo
\mathcal{A}
over finite words
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2021
)
.

4.4.
From Classical
ABW
to
ABW
Modulo
\mathcal{A}

Here we show how a classical
ABW

M=(\Sigma,Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\rho,F)

is lifted to
M_{\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Boolean
algebra over
\Sigma
s.t. for all
a\in\Sigma
there is

\hat{a}\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
s.t.
\llbracket\hat{a}\rrbracket=\{a\}
, and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\forall q\in Q:\varrho(q)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\bigvee_{a\in\Sigma}(\hat{a}\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\rho(q,a))\end{array}

It
follows that for all
q\in Q
and
a\in\Sigma
,

\varrho(q){\downarrow}a=\rho(q,a)
and thus that
M
and

M_{\mathcal{A}}
are equivalent.
A conceptually simple algebra
\mathcal{A}
is the
powerset algebra
(\Sigma,2^{\Sigma},\lambda x.x,\emptyset,\Sigma,\cup,\cap,\lambda x.\Sigma{\setminus}x)
over
\Sigma
where
\hat{a}=\{a\}
.
A more interesting algebra
\mathcal{A}
can be constructed when

\Sigma\subseteq 2^{P}
where
P
is a nonempty finite set of atomic
propositions. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a SAT solver over
P
.
Then

\hat{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}({\Large\mathrel{\&}}_{p\in a}\,p)\mathrel{\&}({\Large\mathrel{\&}}_{p\in P\setminus a}\,{\mathrm{!}}p)
and thus
\llbracket\hat{a}\rrbracket=\{a\}
,
i.e.,
\hat{a}
is intuitively the “minterm” corresponding to
a
.
Note that
\varrho
can also be condensed via rewrites.
For example,

(\hat{a}\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)\lor(\hat{b}\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)\cong(\hat{a}{\mathrel{|}}\hat{b}\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f)
.

5.
Alternation Elimination

Although alternating Büchi automata are an attractive target for
temporal logic, incurring no space blowup, the problem of
testing for nonemptiness is harder for alternating automata
(due to the presence of both conjunction and disjunction in the state
formula) than non-alternating automata
(
Boker et al. 2010
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
be an
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
We introduce an alternation elimination algorithm for
\mathcal{M}
that
is a symbolic derivative-based generalization of the algorithm in
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)

(see also
(
Vardi 1995
, Proposition 20)
).
The algorithm constructs an
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{A},S,S_{0},\sigma,F_{\mathcal{N}})

accepting the same language as
\mathcal{M}
.
As shown by
(
Boker et al. 2010
)
, the concept behind
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
accurately captures
the prime concern in alternation removal, which is the need to associate the
states of the equivalent
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}

\mathcal{N}
with two sets of states from
\mathcal{M}
, which means that
a
\Omega(3^{|Q|})
space blowup cannot be avoided.

In the rest of this section let
\mathbf{Q}=2^{2^{Q}}
,
P=2^{Q}\times 2^{Q}
, and
\mathbf{P}=2^{P}
.
The set
\mathbf{Q}
is the type of leaves of transition terms of
\mathcal{M}
in INF,
i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\varrho}:Q\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{Q}\rangle}
.
The set
\mathbf{P}
is the type of leaves of transition terms of
\mathcal{N}

where
P\supseteq S
is the
state type
of
\mathcal{N}
, i.e.,

\sigma:S\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{P}\rangle}
.

F_{\mathcal{N}}=\{\langle U,V\rangle\in S\mid U=\emptyset\}
.
6
6

6

Here we can replace
\bot
and
\top
with some states
q_{\bot}\in Q{\setminus}F
and
q_{\top}\in F
, with
\varrho(q_{\bot})=q_{\bot}
and
\varrho(q_{\top})=q_{\top}
,
or equivalently, DNF will eliminate
\bot
and
\top
.

5.1.
Algorithm Æ

We start by defining the key operation of
alternation product

of two transition terms
f,g\in{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{Q}\rangle}
as a transition term
f\otimes g\in{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{P}\rangle}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathbf{Q}
and we use (
1
) to lift
\otimes
to

{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{Q}\rangle}\times{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{Q}\rangle}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{P}\rangle}
.

(5)

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\quad}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\psi}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\{\langle X\setminus F,Y\cup(X\cap F)\rangle\mid X\in\boldsymbol{\varphi},Y\in\boldsymbol{\psi}\}\end{array}

The purpose of this construction is to partition the states into
those that have not yet visited a final state (
X\setminus F
) and those that have
(
Y\cup(X\cap F)
).
For
h\in{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbf{P}\rangle}

let
\mathit{States}(h)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bigcup\textit{Leaves}(h)
.
Observe that
\mathit{States}(h)\subseteq P
as it
is the union of all the leaves of
h
where each leaf
is a set of states.

The algorithm Æ constructs an equivalent
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}

\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
from
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
\textit{Dom}(\sigma)
denote the set of
all states in
S
for which the partial transition
function
\sigma
is currently defined in the while loop. Upon
termination
\textit{Dom}(\sigma)=S
.
For
X\subseteq Q
,
let

\boldsymbol{\varrho}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textbf{if}\;X=\emptyset\;\textbf{then}\;\{\emptyset\}\;\textbf{else}\;\bigwedge_{q\in X}\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q)

in:

\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}S_{0}\leftarrow\mathrm{DNF}(Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}})\otimes\{\emptyset\};S\leftarrow S_{0};\sigma\leftarrow\emptyset;\\
\textbf{while}\;\exists\,\langle U,V\rangle\in S\setminus\textit{Dom}(\sigma)\;\textbf{do}\\
\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}l}\textbf{let}&f=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}\boldsymbol{\varrho}(U)\otimes\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V),&\textrm{{if} $U\neq\emptyset$}\\
\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V)\otimes\{\emptyset\},&\textrm{{if} $U=\emptyset$}\end{array}\right\}\end{array}\;\textbf{in}\;\;\sigma(\langle U,V\rangle)\leftarrow f;\;S\leftarrow S\cup\mathit{States}(f)\\
\textbf{return}\;(\mathcal{A},S,S_{0},\sigma,\{\langle U,V\rangle\in S\mid U=\emptyset\})\end{array}\right.

We can make use of the following additional
state reduction
lemma in the algorithm.
The effect of the lemma is already visible in Example
5.1

where we consider a sample run of the algorithm. Without state reduction,
the number of states would roughly
double
in Example
5.1
.

For
\langle U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\rangle,\langle U,V\rangle\in P
let

\langle U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\rangle\subseteq\langle U,V\rangle

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

U^{\prime}\subseteq U
and
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
if
U\neq\emptyset
then
U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 3 (State Reduction).

If
\langle U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\rangle\subseteq\langle U,V\rangle
,
\boldsymbol{\varrho}(U^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(U)
, and

\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V)
,
then
\langle U,V\rangle
can be replaced by
\langle U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\rangle
in the algorithm.

Proof.

Accepting condition is preserved. The computation of
f
preserves
language equivalence using the fact that if we identify states that
have functionally equivalent transition terms and the same acceptance
condition then the language does not change.
∎

Example 5.1.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
in Figure
1
, where
Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}=q_{0}
,
F=\{q_{0},\top\}
, and

\varrho=\{q_{0}\mapsto(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,q_{2}\land q_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{1}\land q_{0}),\quad q_{1}\mapsto(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{1}),\quad q_{2}\mapsto(!\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,q_{2}),\quad\top\mapsto\top\}

one iteration of while-loop in the algorithm.

\mathrm{DNF}(q_{0})\otimes\{\emptyset\}=\{\{q_{0}\}\}\otimes\{\emptyset\}=\{\langle\emptyset,\{q_{0}\}\rangle\}

because
q_{0}\in F
.
