&=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\{\langle\{q_{2}\},\{q_{0}\}\rangle\}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\{\langle\{q_{1}\},\{q_{0}\}\rangle\})&\\
s_{1}=\langle\{q_{1}\},\{q_{0}\}\rangle&\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{1}){\otimes}\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\{\langle\emptyset,\underbrace{\{q_{2},q_{0}\}}_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-16.99998pt]
{\leadsto}\{q_{0}\}\end{array}}\rangle\}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\{\langle\{q_{1}\},\underbrace{\{q_{1},q_{0}\}}_{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-16.99998pt]
{\leadsto}\{q_{0}\}\end{array}}\rangle\})&\\
s_{2}=\langle\{q_{2}\},\{q_{0}\}\rangle&\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{2}){\otimes}\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})=({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\{\langle\emptyset,\{q_{0}\}\rangle\}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\{\langle\{q_{2}\},\{q_{0}\}\rangle\})&\end{array}

The computation of
\sigma(s_{1})
(and
\sigma(s_{2})
)
applied Lemma
3
(indicated by
\leadsto
)
by using that
\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{2})\land\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{1})\land\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{0})
.
The algorithm terminates with the
NBW
shown in the last column.

\Box

5.2.
Correctness of
Æ

The correctness proof of the algorithm is by reduction to
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
,
using generic properties of transition terms and mintermization. In
particular, Theorem
1
implies that

\mathscr{L}(\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right))=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})
. First note that the algorithm terminates
because
P
is finite and
S\subseteq P
.

Let
{\curlywedge}
below denote the
operation that given a pair
\langle U,V\rangle
of finite
sets
U,V
of states, forms their combined conjunction:

{\curlywedge}\langle U,V\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bigwedge(U\cup V)
.
Note that
\bigwedge\!\emptyset=\top
.

Theorem 1 (Æ).

\forall q\in Q_{\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)}
:
\mathscr{L}_{\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)}(q)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}({\curlywedge}q)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}=\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
as above.
We use the following properties:

(i)

the order of members of a conjunction is immaterial due to sets in INF;

(ii)

by definition of INF and Lemma
1
:

\forall f:Q\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)\rangle},a\in\mathbb{D}:\mathit{INF}(f){\downarrow}a=\mathrm{DNF}(f{\downarrow}a)
.

Let
q=\langle U,V\rangle
be such that
U\neq\emptyset
. We get that
(note that
V=\emptyset
implies that
\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V)=\{\emptyset\}
)

(6)

\displaystyle\sigma(\langle U,V\rangle){\downarrow}a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\varrho}(U)\otimes\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V)){\downarrow}a\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{(i)}}}{{=}}\;(\mathit{INF}(\bigwedge_{t\in U}\varrho(t))\otimes\mathit{INF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}\varrho(t))){\downarrow}a

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{(ii)}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in U}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a))\otimes\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:product})}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\{\langle X{\setminus}F,Y\cup(X\cap F)\rangle\mid X\in\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in U}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a)),Y\in\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a))

Now let
q=\langle U,V\rangle
be such that
U=\emptyset
.
We get that

(7)

\displaystyle\sigma(\langle U,V\rangle){\downarrow}a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\varrho}(V)\otimes\{\emptyset\}){\downarrow}a\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{(i)}}}{{=}}\;(\mathit{INF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}\varrho(t))\otimes\{\emptyset\}){\downarrow}a\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{(ii)}}}{{=}}\;\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a))\otimes\{\emptyset\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:product})}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\{\langle X{\setminus}F,X\cap F\rangle\mid X\in\mathrm{DNF}(\bigwedge_{t\in V}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a))\}

Finally, we relate the properties (
6
) and (
7
)
with
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
(recall Section
4.2
), where for
W\subseteq Q
,

\bigwedge_{t\in W}(\varrho(t){\downarrow}a)=\bigwedge_{t\in W}\widehat{\varrho}(t,\widehat{a})
.
The properties (
6
) and (
7
)
represent the construction used in
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)

on top of which further analysis over accepting runs of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}

is built.
Assume, w.l.o.g., that
\textit{Conds}(\sigma)=\textit{Conds}(\varrho)
.
It follows, for all
s\in S
and
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
, that

\begin{array}[]{c}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:mt}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\widehat{w}\in\mathscr{L}_{\widehat{\mathcal{N}}}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{MH84}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\widehat{w}\in\mathscr{L}_{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}({\curlywedge}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:mt}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}({\curlywedge}s)\end{array}

via mintermization and
MH84
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
, and where
we used that minimal satisfiers (
3
) of
\phi
are
included in
\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)
in conditions (
6
) and (
7
).
∎

Observe that
cleaning
of transition terms
constructed by
\otimes

(and that
\mathcal{A}
is
decidable
)
is not necessary for correctness
but essential for any practical implementation of
the algorithm.

5.3.
Product of
NBW
s Modulo
\mathcal{A}
as a Special Case of
Æ

An immediate application of the alternation elimination algorithm
is to construct the
product
of two
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
’s

\mathcal{N}_{i}=(\mathcal{A},Q_{i},Q^{0}_{i},\varrho_{i},F_{i})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
such that
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}\subseteq\{\bot,\top\}
, as the
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{N}_{1}\times\mathcal{N}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{N}_{1}\land\mathcal{N}_{2}\right)\end{array}

Thus, the algorithm provides an essential step in the automata-based approach to
model checking, as discussed in the introduction.
We make some observations of the
properties of Æ in this case that result
in the following corollary of Theorem
1
.
This also implies that the total number of states in
Q_{\mathcal{N}_{1}\times\mathcal{N}_{2}}
is
bounded by
4|Q_{1}||Q_{2}|
.
W.l.o.g., assume also that
\bot
and
\top
do not occur in
Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
.
7
7

7

Or just pretend that
\bot
and
\top
are treated a normal states, this
is just to avoid trivial special cases such as
\langle\{q_{1}\},\emptyset\rangle
.

Corollary 1 (Product).

Let
\langle U,V\rangle\,{\in}\,Q_{\mathcal{N}_{1}{\times}\mathcal{N}_{2}}
. Then

U\,{\cap}\,V=\emptyset
,

U\,{\cap}\,(F_{1}{\cup}F_{2})=\emptyset
, and
there exist
q_{1}\,{\in}\,Q_{1}
and
q_{2}\,{\in}\,Q_{2}

such that

U\,{\cup}\,V=\{q_{1},q_{2}\}

and

\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}_{1}{\times}\mathcal{N}_{2}}(\langle U,V\rangle)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}_{1}}(q_{1})\,{\cap}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}_{2}}(q_{2})
.

A further improvement is that the four cases of
\langle U,V\rangle
that arise in
the corollary can be reduced to two cases by lifting of the classical product of

NBW
s
(
Choueka 1974
)
(see
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 2)
) to modulo
\mathcal{A}
,
which improves the upper bound to
2|Q_{1}||Q_{2}|
.

The construction of product, yet again, demonstrates that
mintermization is avoidable.
Moreover, a classical product
\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{1}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{2}}
is in general
not meaningful

because the respective finite alphabets need not be the same.
One would first need to determine the
shared

alphabet
\textit{Minterms}(\textit{Conds}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\cup\textit{Conds}(\mathcal{N}_{2}))
,
which can have an up-front
exponential
cost.
For example, if
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
each use 10 predicates from
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}

then the shared alphabet can
in the worst case be of size
2^{20}
.

6.
LTL Modulo
\mathcal{A}

Here we lift classical linear temporal logic (LTL) to be modulo

\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{D},\Psi,\llbracket\_\rrbracket,\bot,\top,\mathrel{|},\mathrel{\&},{\mathrm{!}})
as a given
effective Boolean algebra, via symbolic derivatives that translate
LTL into transition terms. We then utilize the
algorithms developed for
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
for decision procedures of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Let
\alpha
range over
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
\varphi,\psi
range over
LTL
.

\varphi\quad::=\quad\alpha\quad\mid\quad\lnot\varphi\quad\mid\quad\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}\quad\mid\quad\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\quad\mid\quad\mathbf{X}\varphi\quad\mid\quad\varphi_{1}\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\varphi_{2}\quad\mid\quad\varphi_{1}\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\varphi_{2}

where
\mathbf{X}
is
Next
,
\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}
is
Until
, and
\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}

is
Release
, are called
modal
formulas. We let the
true

formula be
\top
and
the
false
formula be
\bot
from
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
, and use the following
standard abbreviations:

\varphi\rightarrow\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\lnot\varphi\lor\psi,\quad\mathbf{F}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\top\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi,\quad\mathbf{G}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bot\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi

where
\mathbf{F}
is called
Eventually (or Finally)
and

\mathbf{G}
is called
Globally (or Always)
.

6.1.
Semantics

A word
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is a
model of

\varphi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
,

w\models\varphi
, when the following holds, where
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
:

(8)

\displaystyle w\models\alpha

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w_{0}\in\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket

(9)

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\land\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\models\psi

(10)

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\lor\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w\models\psi

(11)

\displaystyle w\models\lnot\varphi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w\not\models\varphi

(12)

\displaystyle w\models\mathbf{X}\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w_{(1..)}\models\psi

(13)

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\exists j:w_{(j..)}\models\psi\;\textrm{and}\;\forall i{<}j:w_{(i..)}\models\varphi

(14)

\displaystyle w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\forall j:w_{(j..)}\models\psi\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\exists j:w_{(j..)}\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}\forall i{\leq}j:w_{(i..)}\models\psi

(15)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\{w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}\mid w\models\varphi\}

Let
\varphi\equiv\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}(\varphi)=\mathscr{L}(\psi)
.
The rules (
13
) and (
14
) are duals of each other, either one suffices as the
main definition.
Observe that if
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}

then
w\models\lnot\alpha
iff
w\not\models\alpha
iff

w_{0}\notin\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket
iff

w_{0}\in\mathbb{D}\setminus\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket
iff

w\models{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
.
If follows that any Boolean combination of predicates from
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
can itself
be converted into a predicate in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
.

The following examples illustrate some cases of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
modulo
various
\mathcal{A}
. The first example illustrates –

at a very abstract level
– the well-known connection of
integrating SAT solving into
symbolic LTL
, e.g.,
by using BDDs
(
Wulf et al. 2008
)
.

Example 6.1.

Classical LTL over a set of atomic propositions
P
is

\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
where
\mathcal{A}
is an algebra of Boolean combinations over
P
, where
\mathbb{D}=2^{P}

and
\Psi=\mathbb{B}(P)
, and
where satisfiability can be implemented using a SAT solver.
An element
d\in\mathbb{D}
such that
d\vDash\alpha

defines a
truth
assignment
to
P
that makes
\alpha
true. For example, if

P=\{p_{i}\}_{i<7}
and
\alpha=p_{6}\mathrel{\&}p_{5}\mathrel{\&}p_{4}\mathrel{\&}(p_{3}\mathrel{|}(({\mathrm{!}}p_{2})\mathrel{\&}p_{1}))
then if
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is such
that
w(0)=\{p_{1},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}\}
and
w(1)=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}\}

then
w(0)\models\alpha
but
w(1)\not\models\alpha
.
Thus, e.g.,
w\not\models\mathbf{G}\alpha
.

\Box

While in classical LTL, as in Example
6.1
,

\mathbb{D}
is
finite
,
in the next example
\mathbb{D}
is
infinite
.

Example 6.2.

Consider LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
as
the algebra of Satisfiability Modulo Theories formulas
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}

that can be implemented using an SMT solver.
In this case
\mathbb{D}
is the set of models that provide interpretations to
uninterpreted constants
or
variables
.
Let
\beta
be the predicate
0<x
and let
\alpha
be
the predicate
x<1
where
x
is a variable of type
real
.
Then
\alpha\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\beta
states that
x

must remain positive until
x<1
.
E.g.,
(x{\mapsto}1)(x{\mapsto}\frac{1}{2})(x{\mapsto}0)^{\omega}\models\alpha\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\beta
.

\Box

6.2.
Vardi Derivatives of
LTL
Lifted to Modulo
\mathcal{A}

Here we show how the semantics of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be realized via transition terms.
The key observation here is that
a concrete derivative (i.e., for a given symbol
a\in\mathbb{D}
)
is not actually constructed but
maintained in a symbolic form as a transition term.
Let
\alpha
range over
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
, and
\varphi
and
\psi
range over
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
The
symbolic derivative
of an
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
formula is defined as
the following transition term in
{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle{\textit{LTL}}\rangle}
:

(16)

\displaystyle\delta(\alpha)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top)

(17)

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi\land\psi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi)\land\delta(\psi)

(18)

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi\lor\psi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi)\lor\delta(\psi)

(19)

\displaystyle\delta(\lnot\varphi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\lnot\delta(\varphi)

(20)

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{X}\psi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\psi

(21)

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\psi)\lor(\delta(\varphi)\land(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))

(22)

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\psi)\land(\delta(\varphi)\lor(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi))

Symbolic derivatives, as given above, lift the construction in
(
Vardi 1995
)

(see also
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 24)
) as to be modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

We also let
\delta(\top)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\top
and
\delta(\bot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\bot
and
observe that
\lnot\top\equiv\bot
and
\lnot\bot\equiv\top
.
We use the following properties of

\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{F}
(where
\bot\lor\phi\cong\phi
and

\top\land\phi\cong\phi
are applied implicitly):

\delta(\mathbf{G}\psi)\cong\delta(\psi)\land\mathbf{G}\psi\qquad\delta(\mathbf{F}\psi)\cong\delta(\psi)\lor\mathbf{F}\psi

Correctness of the derivation rules will follow as a special case
of Theorem
4
(presented in Section
7
).

We now link derivatives formally with
ABW
s.
We write
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
for the
positive
formulas in

\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
where
\lnot
does not occur.
This is a standard normal form assumption and every formula
in
LTL
can be translated into

\textit{LTL}^{+}
of the same size if members of
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}

are treated as units. The particular aspect with modulo
\mathcal{A}
is that

complement is propagated into
\mathcal{A}
, i.e., for
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
,
the formula
\lnot\alpha
becomes the predicate
{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
.

Definition 6.3 (
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
).

The
positive fragment of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
is obtained by
eliminating any explicit use of
\lnot
through de Morgan’s laws
and the standard equivalence preserving
rules where
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
\varphi,\psi\in\textit{RLTL}
:

\lnot\alpha\equiv{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
,

\lnot\mathbf{X}\varphi\equiv\mathbf{X}\lnot\varphi
,

\lnot(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi)\equiv\lnot\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\lnot\psi
,
and

\lnot(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi)\equiv\lnot\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\lnot\psi
.

Definition 6.4 (
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
for
\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
).

For
\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
let
Q_{\phi}
denote the set
containing
\bot
and
\top
and
all
non-Boolean
subformulas of
\phi
, i.e., predicates in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
,
and all
modal
formulas.
Let
\varrho_{\phi}
denote
\lambda q.\delta(q)
for
q\in Q_{\phi}
.
Let
F_{\phi}
contain all the
\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}
-formulas and
\top
in
Q_{\phi}
.
Then

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{A},Q_{\phi},\phi,\varrho_{\phi},F_{\phi}).

Observe that
\varrho_{\phi}:Q_{\phi}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q_{\phi})\rangle}
.

We now state a key result from
(
Vardi 1995
)

(
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 24)
) lifted as to be modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem 2 (Vardi derivatives modulo theories).

\forall\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}:\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{\phi})=\mathscr{L}(\phi)

Theorem
2
implies that the more general language invariant
is preserved by
all
states of
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
.

Corollary 2 (LTL invariance).

\forall\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}:\forall\psi\in Q_{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}}:\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}}(\psi)=\mathscr{L}(\psi)

Proof.

\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}}(\psi){=}\mathscr{L}({\mathcal{M}_{\phi}^{(\psi)}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}({\mathcal{M}_{\psi}}){=}\mathscr{L}(\psi)

where
(\star)
holds because only
Q_{\psi}
matters.
∎

6.3.
Examples

Example 6.5.

Let
\phi=\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)
, where

\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
is such that
\alpha\nequiv\bot
and
\alpha\nequiv\top
.
Then the transition function
\varrho=\varrho_{\phi}
has the following (relevant) transition terms
in addition to
\varrho(\top)=\top
:

\varrho(\mathbf{F}\alpha)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha),\quad\varrho(\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)=({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha),\quad\varrho(\phi)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\phi)

where

\delta(\mathbf{F}\alpha)\cong(\delta(\alpha)\lor\mathbf{F}\alpha)=((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top)\lor\mathbf{F}\alpha)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\lor\mathbf{F}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot\lor\mathbf{F}\alpha)\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha)
and similarly that
\delta(\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)\cong({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)
, and

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\delta(\phi)&\cong&(\delta(\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)\land\phi)=(\delta(\mathbf{F}\alpha)\land\delta(\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)\land\phi)\cong((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha)\land({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)\land\phi)\\
&\cong&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,({\mathrm{!}}\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi))\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\phi)\end{array}

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
is shown in Figure
1

with
q_{0}=\phi
,
q_{1}=\mathbf{F}\alpha
and
q_{2}=\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
.

\Box

Note that some states may become
irrelevant
(unreachable from
\phi
) through
rewrites of
\varrho_{\phi}
, which the abstract definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
does not
directly reflect, e.g.,
\alpha
and
{\mathrm{!}}\alpha
are not relevant as states in
Example
6.5
above.
The following example illustrates the impact
of cleaning modulo different element theories
\mathcal{A}
and
how this is reflected in the computation of
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
.

Example 6.6.

Consider
\mathcal{A}
as an SMT solver, let
x
be of type
integer
, and
let
\phi=(x{<}1)\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}(0{<}x)
, stating that the condition that
x
must be positive
is released when
x<1
becomes true. Then

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\delta(\phi)&\cong&\delta(0{<}x)\land(\delta(x{<}1)\lor\phi)=(0{<}x\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\land((x{<}1\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\lor\phi)\\
&\cong&(0{<}x\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\land(x{<}1\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\phi)\cong(0{<}x\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(x{<}1\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\phi)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\cong(0{<}x\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\end{array}

where
\textit{UNSAT}((0{<}x){\mathrel{\&}}(x{<}1))
is used for cleaning.
Essentially
(x{<}1)\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}(0{<}x)
becomes

\mathbf{G}(0{<}x)
.

\Box

6.4.
Alternation Elimination for
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}

Combined together, Theorem
1
and
Corollary
2
show
the decidability of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
for effective
\mathcal{A}

and allow us to directly use the alternation
elimination algorithm (modulo
\mathcal{A}
) on the transition terms
resulting from the symbolic derivatives of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}

to produce an nondeterministic Büchi automata from
an LTL formula in a lazy manner, while safely applying
many LTL-based rewrites.

Theorem 3 (LTL invariance of Æ).

\forall\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}:\forall q\in Q_{\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\right)}:\mathscr{L}_{\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\right)}(q)=\mathscr{L}({\curlywedge}q)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
and
\mathcal{N}=\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.
Fix
q=\langle U,V\rangle\in Q_{\mathcal{N}}
. We show that
\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(q)=\mathscr{L}({\curlywedge}q)
.
We have that

\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{N}}(q)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}({\curlywedge}q)=\bigcap_{\psi\in U\cup V}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\psi)=\bigcap_{\psi\in U\cup V}\mathscr{L}(\psi)=\mathscr{L}({\curlywedge}q)

where the first equality holds by Theorem
1
and
the third equality holds by Corollary
2

because
U\cup V\subseteq Q_{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

Many optimizations can be applied to states
\langle U,V\rangle
in

Q_{\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\right)}
above in accordance with
Lemma
3
. For example, for any
\varphi
, if

\varphi,\mathbf{G}(\varphi\land\_)\in V
then
\varphi
can be
eliminated from
V
: observe that trivially

\delta(\varphi\land\mathbf{G}(\varphi\land\_))\cong\delta(\mathbf{G}(\varphi\land\_))
,
which means that the derivative (transition term) does not even need
to be computed in this case, in order to know that
Lemma
3
can be applied. In fact, this exact
situation appeared twice implicitly in
Example
5.1
. Many similar rules can be used,
where the one illustrated here, is an instance of the subsumption rule
in
(
Somenzi and Bloem 2000
,
\leq
)
. Such rules would clearly be out of
reach if the LTL invariance property had been lost in translation.
A full analysis of which rules in
(
Somenzi and Bloem 2000
)
preserve
LTL invariance of Æ is beyond the scope of this work.

7.
LTL with Extended Regular Expressions Modulo
\mathcal{A}

We now show the power of symbol derivatives
by using them to combine the following two languages into one:

•

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
, extended regular expressions modulo
\mathcal{A}
, as given by
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
, summarized
in Section
7.1
;

•

\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
, as given in the previous section.

We first work with
finite
words in
\mathbb{D}^{*}
, and later
lift the semantics to
infinite
words in
\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
when
we extend
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
with
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
in the combined language

\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
(Section
7.2
)

Our focus here is on the
classical subset
of the extended regular expression
operators supported in SPOT
(
Duret-Lutz 2022
)
and PSL
(
Eisner and Fisman 2006
)
.
8
8

8

Many other
operators, such as
fusion
,
non-length-matching
intersection
and
bounded iteration
can also be supported.

The language
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
has regex
complement
, which is not in SPOT/PSL
but can be supported naturally with transition terms.

Finally, we lift
\omega
-regular languages to be modulo
\mathcal{A}
and show that

\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
precisely captures
\omega
-regularity modulo
\mathcal{A}
(Section
7.3
).

7.1.
Extended Regular Expressions Modulo
\mathcal{A}
and Their Derivatives

We fix
\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{D},\Psi,\llbracket\_\rrbracket,\bot,\top,\mathrel{|},\mathrel{\&},{\mathrm{!}})
.

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
\mathcal{R}
for short is defined by the following abstract grammar, where
\alpha
ranges
over
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
. We let
R
range over
\mathcal{R}
,
R
is called a
regex
.
We write
\mathcal{R}^{\textsc{std}}_{\mathcal{A}}
for the
standard
fragment of
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
without
\land
or
˜
.

R\quad::=\quad\alpha\quad|\quad(\hskip-1.19995pt)\quad|\quad R_{1}\lor R_{2}\quad|\quad R_{1}\land R_{2}\quad|\quad R_{1}\cdot R_{2}\quad|\quad R{\ast}\quad|\quad\texttt{\char 126\relax}R

Here
(\hskip-1.19995pt)
stands for the regex that accepts
the empty word

\epsilon\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
.
We reuse
\bot
from
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
for the regex that that
accepts
nothing
(
the empty language
). The operators
appear in order of precedence, with

union

(\lor)
having lowest and
complement

(\texttt{\char 126\relax})
having highest precedence.
The remaining operators are
intersection

(\land)
,

concatenation

(\cdot)
,
and
Kleene star

({\ast})
.
We also let
R{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}R{\cdot}R{\ast}
.
We often abbreviate concatenation by juxtaposition.
Then
(\mathbb{D}^{*},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{L},\bot,\top{\ast},\lor,\land,\texttt{\char 126\relax})

is the associated effective Boolean algebra,
where, for
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
, the following additional laws hold:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\qquad\mathcal{L}((\hskip-1.19995pt))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{\epsilon\}\qquad\mathcal{L}(R\cdot R^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(R){\cdot}\mathcal{L}(R^{\prime})\qquad\mathcal{L}(R{\ast})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(R)^{*}\end{array}

Observe therefore that
\mathcal{L}(\texttt{\char 126\relax}\bot)=\mathcal{L}(\top{\ast})=\mathbb{D}^{*}
while

\mathcal{L}({\mathrm{!}}\bot)=\mathcal{L}(\top)=\mathbb{D}
.
The following operation, called
nullability
of a regex
R
, is
defined recursively to decide if
\epsilon\in\mathcal{L}(R)
:
9
9

9

Nullability
can be maintained as a Boolean flag associated with the abstract syntax tree
node of every regex that is maintained at construction time of regexes and
therefore nullability test is a trivial constant time operation at runtime.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}lr@{\;}c@{\;}lr@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\mathit{Null}((\hskip-1.19995pt))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{true}&\mathit{Null}(R{\ast})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{true}&\mathit{Null}(R\lor R^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathit{Null}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\mathit{Null}(R^{\prime})\\
&&&\mathit{Null}(\alpha)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{false}&\mathit{Null}(R\land R^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathit{Null}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}\mathit{Null}(R^{\prime})\\
&&&\mathit{Null}(\texttt{\char 126\relax}R)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathbf{not}\,\mathit{Null}(R)&\mathit{Null}(R\cdot R^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathit{Null}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}\mathit{Null}(R^{\prime})\\
\end{array}

We now recall
the definition of
transition regexes
from
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
10
10

10

Here
we use
\partial
not to overload
\delta
and to avoid ambiguity because

\partial(\alpha)\neq\delta(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
.

(23)

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\;}r@{\;}c@{\;}ll@{}}\partial((\hskip-1.19995pt))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\bot&\partial(\texttt{\char 126\relax}R)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\texttt{\char 126\relax}\partial(R)&\hbox{\multirowsetup\mbox{$\partial(R{\cdot}R^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}\partial(R){\cdot}R^{\prime}\,{\lor}\,\partial(R^{\prime}),&\textrm{{if} $\mathit{Null}(R)$;}\\
\partial(R){\cdot}R^{\prime},&\textrm{{otherwise}.}\end{array}\right.$}}\\
\partial(\alpha)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt))&\partial(R\,{\lor}\,R^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\partial(R)\,{\lor}\,\partial(R^{\prime})\\
\partial(R{\ast})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\partial(R){\cdot}R{\ast}&\partial(R\,{\land}\,R^{\prime})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\partial(R)\,{\land}\,\partial(R^{\prime})\end{array}

where
\lor
and
\land
are lifted to transition terms in
{\mathcal{T}\langle\mathcal{R}\rangle}
by
(
1
), and
\lambda x.x{\cdot}r
and
˜
are lifted
by (
2
). We use Lemma
4
that follows
from
(
Stanford et al. 2021
, Theorem 4.3 and Theorem 7.1)
. In this
context functional equivalence
f\cong g
based rewrite rules of
transition regexes is also crucial for termination of the resulting
DFA
\mathcal{M}_{R}
defined below. In addition to
associativity,
commutativity and idempotence
of
\lor
and
\land
,
de Morgan’s laws are used to propagate
˜
, and concatenation is
an
associative operator with
(\hskip-1.19995pt)
as unit and
\bot
as zero
.
Most of these rules are
mandatory
for
\mathcal{M}_{R}
to
be
finite
and several others are practically
very useful to further reduce the state space. E.g.,

\texttt{\char 126\relax}\bot\cong\top{\ast}
and
\texttt{\char 126\relax}(\hskip-1.19995pt)\cong\top{+}
.

Definition 7.1 (
\mathcal{M}_{R}
for
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
).

\mathcal{M}_{R}=(\mathcal{A},Q,R,\varrho,F)
where

Q\subset\mathcal{R}
is finite,
R\in Q
is the initial state,

\varrho:Q\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}\langle Q\rangle}
is the transition function such that

\varrho(q)\cong\partial(q)
, and
F=\{q\in Q\mid\mathit{Null}(q)\}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
.
For
u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
and
q\in Q
, let
\hat{\varrho}(u,q)
be the state reached from
q
by
u
.

\hat{\varrho}(u,q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textbf{if}\;u=\epsilon\;\textbf{then}\;q\;\textbf{else}\;\hat{\varrho}(u_{(1..)},\varrho(q){\downarrow}u_{0})

The
language of
\mathcal{M}
for
q\in Q
is then

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}\mid\hat{\varrho}(u,q)\in F\}
.

Lemma 4.

For
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{R}
as above,

\forall q\in Q,a\in\mathbb{D}:\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)=\mathcal{L}(q)\;\textrm{and}\;\mathcal{L}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)=\mathbf{D}_{a}(\mathcal{L}(q))
.

A state
r
is called
alive
if
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(r)\neq\emptyset
else
dead
.
Observe that for
\mathcal{M}_{R}
, a state

\hat{\varrho}(u,R)
is alive iff
\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R)
, i.e., the regex reached from
R
via
u
accepts
some
x\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
.

Let
\mathit{One}(R)
denote the condition in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
that corresponds to

\mathcal{L}(R)\cap\mathbb{D}
. Formally, let
R,r\in\mathcal{R}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{One}(R)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathit{One}_{\top}(\partial(R))\\
\mathit{One}_{\beta}(r)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\textbf{if}\;\mathit{Null}(r)\;\textbf{then}\;\beta\;\textbf{else}\;\bot\\
\mathit{One}_{\beta}((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}&\mathit{One}_{\beta\mathrel{\&}\alpha}(f)\mathrel{|}\mathit{One}_{\beta\mathrel{\&}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha}(g)\end{array}

Moreover, normalize
\mathit{One}(R)
to
\bot
if
\mathit{One}(R)\equiv\bot
and to
\top
if

\mathit{One}(R)\equiv\top
.

7.2.
LTL Combined with Extended Regular Expressions Modulo
\mathcal{A}

Here we consider the following extension
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
of
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
that combines

\mathcal{R}
with
LTL
. Let
\phi
range over
RLTL
and let
R
range over
\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\alpha
range over
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
.

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle::=

\displaystyle\alpha\;\mid\;\lnot\phi\;\mid\;\phi\lor\phi^{\prime}\;\mid\;\phi\land\phi^{\prime}\;\mid\;\mathbf{X}\phi\;\mid\;\phi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\phi^{\prime}\;\mid\;\phi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\phi^{\prime}\;\mid\;

\displaystyle R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\;\mid\;R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\;\mid\;\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\;\mid\;\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\;\mid\;R^{\omega}

The operator

\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}
is
existential suffix implication
,

\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}
is
universal suffix implication
,

\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
is the
weak closure
of
R
,

\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
is the
negated weak closure
of
R
,
and

R^{\omega}
is the
\omega
-closure
of
R
.
The semantics of
\phi\in\textit{RLTL}
is
a conservative extension of
LTL
and the
semantics of the new formulas is consistent
with the semantics in
(
Duret-Lutz 2022
, 2.6.1)
, except that if
R
is
nullable

then in
RLTL
the formula
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
) is equivalent to
\top

(resp.
\bot
) and
R^{\omega}
is not supported in SPOT.
11
11

11

In other words,

\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
) in SPOT corresponds in
RLTL

to
\boldsymbol{\{}R\land\top{+}\boldsymbol{\}}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{!\{}R\land\top{+}\boldsymbol{\}}
).

Let
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
.

(24)

\displaystyle w\models R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\exists\,u,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models\phi

(25)

\displaystyle w\models R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\forall\,u,v:\textbf{if}\;w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\;\textbf{then}\;v\models\phi

(26)

\displaystyle w\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(\exists\,u\prec_{\epsilon}w:u\in\mathcal{L}(R))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(\forall\,u\prec w:\exists\,x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R))

(27)

\displaystyle w\models\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w\not\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}

(28)

\displaystyle w\models R^{\omega}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle w\in(\mathcal{L}(R)\setminus\{\epsilon\})^{\omega}

Definitions (
8
)-(
15
) carry over to
RLTL
.
For
\phi,\psi\in\textit{RLTL}
let
\phi\equiv\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathscr{L}(\phi)=\mathscr{L}(\psi)
.
Observe that if
R
is nullable in (
26
) then

u=\epsilon\in\mathcal{L}(R)
and thus
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\top
and

\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\bot
by (
27
).
It follows from the definitions that
R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\equiv\lnot(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\lnot\phi)

and trivially that
\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\lnot\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
. These formulas play a useful
role in the positive fragment of
RLTL
that we denote by
\textit{RLTL}^{+}
.

Definition 7.2 (
\textit{RLTL}^{+}
).

The
positive fragment of
RLTL
is obtained by
using the rules in Definition
6.3
, and for
R\in\mathcal{R}
and
\varphi,\psi\in\textit{RLTL}
:

\lnot(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\varphi)\equiv R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\lnot\varphi
,

\lnot(R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\varphi)\equiv R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\lnot\varphi
,

\lnot\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
, and

\lnot\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
.
Moreover,
\lnot R^{\omega}
is not permitted in
\textit{RLTL}^{+}
.

We now define transition terms for
RLTL
as members of
{\mathcal{T}\langle\textit{RLTL}\rangle}
.
Derivative rules
(
16
)–(
22
) carry over to
RLTL
and we add the
following new rules:

(29)

\displaystyle\delta(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi)\lor(\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)

(30)

\displaystyle\delta(R\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\rightarrow\phi)\land(\partial(R)\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)

(31)

\displaystyle\delta(\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\textbf{if}\;\mathit{Null}(R)\;\textbf{then}\;\top\;\textbf{else}\;\boldsymbol{\{}\partial(R)\boldsymbol{\}}

(32)

\displaystyle\delta(\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\textbf{if}\;\mathit{Null}(R)\;\textbf{then}\;\bot\;\textbf{else}\;\boldsymbol{!\{}\partial(R)\boldsymbol{\}}

(33)

\displaystyle\delta(R^{\omega})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\delta(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega})

where

r\,{\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\,\phi
,

r\,{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\,\phi
,

\boldsymbol{\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}
, and

\boldsymbol{!\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}

are lifted to

{\mathcal{T}\langle\textit{RLTL}\rangle}
by (
2
).
For
u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
and
\phi\in\textit{RLTL}
let

\hat{\delta}(u,\phi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\textbf{if}\;u=\epsilon\;\textbf{then}\;\phi\;\textbf{else}\;\hat{\delta}(u_{(1..)},\delta(\phi){\downarrow}u_{0}).

Rules (
29
)–(
32
) seamlessly combine
the derivatives of
LTL
(
16
)–(
22
) with
derivatives of
\mathcal{R}
(
23
). Correctness of the combined
rule-set is proved in Theorem
4
. Note that if

\mathit{One}(R)=\bot
then the lhs disjunct of (
29
) reduces
to
\bot
and the lhs conjunct of (
30
) reduces to

\top
. Moreover, if
\partial(R)=\bot
or
\partial(R)=(\hskip-1.19995pt)
then the rhs
disjunct of (
29
) reduces
\bot
and the rhs conjunct
of (
30
) reduces to
\top
, using
new
rewrite
rules in
RLTL
: for
r=\bot
or
r=(\hskip-1.19995pt)
, we let

r\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\cong\bot
and
r\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\cong\top
. Furthermore,

\boldsymbol{\{}\bot\boldsymbol{\}}\cong\bot
and

\boldsymbol{!\{}\bot\boldsymbol{\}}\cong\top
,
and if
r
is nullable then
\boldsymbol{\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}\cong\top

and
\boldsymbol{!\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}\cong\bot
.
Observe that all these rules are consistent with
the semantics of
RLTL
.

Let
\textit{Acc}(\textit{RLTL}^{+})
denote the subset of all the following formulas
of
\textit{RLTL}^{+}
:
\top
, all
\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}
formulas,
all
\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}
formulas,
all
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
formulas where
R
is
alive
,
all
\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
formulas where
R
is
dead
,
and all
R^{\omega}
formulas.

Definition 7.3 (
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
for
\phi\in\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
).

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
is the
ABW

(\mathcal{A},Q,\phi,\varrho,Q\cap\textit{Acc}(\textit{RLTL}^{+}))

where
Q\subseteq\textit{RLTL}^{+}
is the least set of states
such that
\phi\in Q
and if

q\in Q
then
\varrho(q)\cong\delta(q)
and
if
p
is a non-Boolean subformula of a leaf of
\varrho(q)
then
p\in Q
.

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
is well-defined (
Q
above is finite)
because all the
\mathcal{M}_{R}
are finite where
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
by Lemma
4
.
In contrast to
LTL
derivatives, where all the original subformulas are enough,
for
\phi\in\textit{RLTL}
, in
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
we also get new formulas in suffix implications and closures, e.g.,
in every leaf
r\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\psi
of
\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\psi
,
r
is some derivative of
R
and

r\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\psi
is typically not a subformula of the original formula
\phi
.

Example 7.4.

Consider the
RLTL
formula
\psi=\alpha\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi

with
\phi=\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha)
.
Then
\partial(\alpha)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt))
. Since
\mathit{One}(\alpha)=\alpha
, by using (
29
),
we get that, recall also the derivatives from Example
6.5
,

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l}q&\delta(q)&\textit{ABW}\;\mathcal{M}_{\psi}\\
\hline\cr\psi&\delta(\alpha\land\phi)\lor(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)&\hbox{\multirowsetup

&\cong\delta(\alpha\land\phi)\lor(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\bot)&\\
&\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top)\land\delta(\phi)&\\
&\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\mathbf{F}\alpha\land\phi))&\\
&\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{F}{\mathrm{!}}\alpha\land\phi)&\end{array}

The resulting
ABW
requires alternation elimination for
NBW
conversion.

\Box

Example 7.5.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and consider

\phi=(\alpha\beta){+}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma
. First we
construct the transition terms of
\mathcal{M}_{(\alpha\beta){+}}

for all
r\in Q_{\mathcal{M}}
.
Note that
(\hskip-1.19995pt)
(resp.
\bot
) is the unit (resp. zero) of concatenation.
For example,
(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt)){\cdot}{\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast}}=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt)\cdot\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot\cdot\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast})\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast})
.

r_{1}=\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast}&(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha\beta){\ast})&\beta&\mathbf{false}&\\
r_{2}=(\alpha\beta){\ast}&\partial(\alpha\beta){\cdot}(\alpha\beta){\ast}=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta){\cdot}(\alpha\beta){\ast}\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta(\alpha\beta){\ast})&\bot&\mathbf{true}&\end{array}

The transition terms that arise from
\phi

give rise here to the
ABW

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
as shown below.
Observe that
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
also happens to be an
NBW
because
conjunctions do not arise:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l|l|l@{}}q&\delta(q)&\textit{ABW}\;(\textit{NBW})\;\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\\

q_{1}=r_{1}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma&\delta(\beta\land\mathbf{G}\gamma)\lor(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma)&\\
&\cong(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\gamma\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}\gamma)\lor(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma)\\
&\cong(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\gamma\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}\gamma\lor(r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma))\\
q_{2}=r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,r_{1}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma)&\\
q_{3}=\mathbf{G}\gamma&(\gamma\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}\gamma)&\end{array}

Note that the guard
\beta
of the transition
{q_{1}}\,{\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.00006pt}{\scriptsize$\xrightarrow{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\beta\\[-3.27773pt]
\end{array}}$}}}\,{q_{2}}

is a simplification of
(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\gamma){\mathrel{|}}({\mathrm{!}}(\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\gamma){\mathrel{\&}}\beta)
.
The automaton is thus truly
nondeterministic
because the outgoing transition guards from

q_{1}
overlap, in other words, the leaf

\mathbf{G}\gamma\lor(r_{2}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}\gamma)=q_{3}\lor q_{2}
of
\delta(q_{1})
is reachable
when
\beta{\mathrel{\&}}\gamma\nequiv\bot
.

\Box

Example 7.6.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be predicates such that
\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket=\{a\}

and
\llbracket\beta\rrbracket=\{b\}
where
a\neq b
.
Let
R={(\alpha{\cdot}\top){\ast}{\cdot}\beta}
.
The following table shows the
transitions that arise in the
DBW

\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
,
where
\textit{UNSAT}(\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\beta)
is used to clean
\delta(\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}})
.
Observe that
{\mathrm{!}}\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\beta\equiv\beta
.
The table also shows
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
where

\boldsymbol{!\{}(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt))\boldsymbol{\}}=(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\boldsymbol{!\{}(\hskip-1.19995pt)\boldsymbol{\}}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\boldsymbol{!\{}\bot\boldsymbol{\}})\cong(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\bot\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\top)\cong({\mathrm{!}}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top)
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l|l|l|l@{}}q&\delta(q)&\textit{DBW}\;\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}&\textit{DBW}\;\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\boldsymbol{\{}\top{\cdot}R\boldsymbol{\}}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\boldsymbol{\{}(\hskip-1.19995pt)\boldsymbol{\}}))&\hbox{\multirowsetup

\small\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\boldsymbol{\{}\top{\cdot}R\boldsymbol{\}}&(\top\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}})\cong\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}&&\\
\top(\cong\boldsymbol{\{}(\hskip-1.19995pt)\boldsymbol{\}})&\top&&\end{array}

Then
a^{\omega}\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
because
\forall u\prec a^{\omega}:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R)
.
Note also that

\mathscr{L}(\mathbf{G}({\mathrm{!}}\beta)\land\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}})=\mathscr{L}((\alpha{\mathrm{!}}\beta)^{\omega})

where
\alpha
holds in all even positions – a classical example
not expressible in LTL
(
Wolper 1983
)
.

&\cong((\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)&\\
&\cong(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\beta\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)&\\
q_{1}=\beta\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi&\delta(\beta\land\mathbf{X}\phi)\lor(\partial(\beta)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)&\beta\\
&=\delta(\beta)\land\delta(\mathbf{X}\phi)\lor((\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt))\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)&\\
&=((\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top)\land\phi)\lor(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\hskip-1.19995pt)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)&\\
&\cong(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\phi)\lor(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\bot\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\cong(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\phi)\lor\bot\cong(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\phi)&\end{array}

Observe that
(\alpha\beta)^{\omega}
is equivalent with

\mathbf{G}(\alpha{\mathrel{|}}\beta)\land\boldsymbol{\{}(\alpha\beta){\ast}{\cdot}{\mathrm{!}}(\alpha{\mathrel{|}}\beta)\boldsymbol{\}}

if
{\mathrm{!}}(\alpha{\mathrel{|}}\beta)
is satisfiable.

\Box

Theorem
4
describes the semantics of
RLTL

in terms of languages and derivatives. It
shows that the definition of derivatives correctly captures the
intended semantics. Observe that this theorem does not yet imply any formal
relationship with the corresponding
ABW
semantics that arises, that is discussed
in the next section.
For
L\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{*}
, let

\textit{MinSet}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\{u\in L\mid\not\exists x\prec_{\epsilon}u:x\in L\}

i.e.,
\textit{MinSet}(L)
denotes the set of all words in
L

that are
minimal
in the sense that they have no proper prefixes in
L
.
In particular,
\mathit{Null}(R)\Leftrightarrow\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(R))=\{\epsilon\}
.
Theorem
4
uses Lemma
5
.

Lemma 5.

Let
R\in\mathcal{R}
,
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
, and
a\in\mathbb{D}
. Then

(1)

{w\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\Leftrightarrow(\exists u{\prec_{\epsilon}}w:u\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(R)))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(\forall u{\prec}w:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R))}

(2)

If
R
is not nullable then

\forall u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}:{au\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(R))\Leftrightarrow u\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a))}

(3)

If
R
is not nullable then

(\forall u\prec aw:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R))\Leftrightarrow(\forall u\prec w:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a))

Proof.

Using that
\exists\,u\prec_{\epsilon}w:u\in\mathcal{L}(R)\Leftrightarrow\exists u\prec_{\epsilon}w:u\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(R))
, and that

au\in\mathcal{L}(R)\Leftrightarrow u\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)
.
In (
3
) note that
ux\in\mathcal{L}(R)
iff

u_{(1..)}x\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}u_{0})
where
a=u_{0}
.
The case of
\exists x:ax\in\mathcal{L}(R)

in the direction ‘
\Leftarrow
’
is implied in (
3
)
by
\partial(R){\downarrow}a
being alive.
∎

The following lemma is also used. While it is a well-known fact for
LTL here we show that it also holds in
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
and is independent of
\mathbb{D}
.

Lemma 6.

\forall\varphi,\psi\in\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
:
1)

\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi\equiv\psi\lor(\varphi\land\mathbf{X}(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))

and
2)

\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi\equiv\psi\land(\varphi\lor\mathbf{X}(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}\psi))

Theorem 4 (Derivation).

\forall\phi\in\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}},a\in\mathbb{D},w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}:aw\models\phi\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\phi){\downarrow}a
.

Proof.

Fix
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
and
a\in\mathbb{D}
.
We prove the statement by
by induction over
\phi
.

Case
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
:

aw\models\alpha\Leftrightarrow a\in\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow w\models(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\top\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot){\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\alpha){\downarrow}a
.

Case
\mathbf{X}\varphi
:

aw\models\mathbf{X}\varphi\Leftrightarrow w\models\varphi\Leftrightarrow w\models\varphi{\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\mathbf{X}\varphi){\downarrow}a
.

Case
\varphi\lor\psi
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}lc@{\;}l@{}}aw\models\varphi\lor\psi&\Leftrightarrow&aw\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}aw\models\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\models\delta(\varphi){\downarrow}a\lor\delta(\psi){\downarrow}a\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models(\delta(\varphi)\lor\delta(\psi)){\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\varphi\lor\psi){\downarrow}a\end{array}

Case
\lnot\varphi
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}lc@{\;}l@{}}aw\models\lnot\varphi&\Leftrightarrow&aw\not\models\varphi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\not\models\delta(\varphi){\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\lnot(\delta(\varphi){\downarrow}a)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models(\lnot\delta(\varphi)){\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\lnot\varphi){\downarrow}a\end{array}

Case
\phi=\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}aw\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:UR}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&aw\models\psi\lor(\varphi\land\mathbf{X}(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))\Leftrightarrow aw\models\psi\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(aw\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models\delta(\psi){\downarrow}a\lor(\delta(\varphi){\downarrow}a\land(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\models(\delta(\psi)\lor(\delta(\varphi)\land(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi))){\downarrow}a\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models\delta(\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi){\downarrow}a\end{array}

Case
R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}aw\models R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi&\Leftrightarrow&\exists u,v:aw=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models\phi\\
&\Leftrightarrow&(a\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}aw\models\phi)\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\exists u,v:aw=auv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}auv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models\phi\\
&\Leftrightarrow&(aw\models\mathit{One}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}aw\models\phi)\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\exists u,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models\phi\\
&\Leftrightarrow&aw\models\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w\models\partial(R){\downarrow}a\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi){\downarrow}a\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w\models\partial(R){\downarrow}a\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi){\downarrow}a\lor(\partial(R){\downarrow}a\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models(\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi)\lor\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi){\downarrow}a\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models\delta(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi){\downarrow}a\end{array}

Case
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
: If
\mathit{Null}(R)=\mathbf{true}
then

aw\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\;\Leftrightarrow\;aw\models\top\;\Leftrightarrow\;w\models\top{\downarrow}a\;\Leftrightarrow\;w\models\delta(\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}){\downarrow}a.

Let
\mathit{Null}(R)=\mathbf{false}
then

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}aw\models\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\textrm{\ref{lma:MinPrefix}(\ref{lma:MinPrefix:1})}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&(\exists u\prec_{\epsilon}aw:u\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(R)))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(\forall u\prec aw:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(R))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\textrm{\ref{lma:MinPrefix}(\ref{lma:MinPrefix:2})},\textrm{\ref{lma:MinPrefix}(\ref{lma:MinPrefix:3})}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&(\exists u\prec_{\epsilon}w:u\in\textit{MinSet}(\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}(\forall u\prec w:\exists x:ux\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\textrm{\ref{lma:MinPrefix}(\ref{lma:MinPrefix:1})}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models\boldsymbol{\{}\partial(R){\downarrow}a\boldsymbol{\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\models\boldsymbol{\{}\partial(R)\boldsymbol{\}}{\downarrow}a\Leftrightarrow w\models\delta(\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}){\downarrow}a\end{array}

Case
R^{\omega}
: We use that

a\in\mathcal{L}(R)\Leftrightarrow\epsilon\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)
. Recall
also that, for all
v\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
,
v\models\mathbf{X}\varphi\Leftrightarrow v_{(1..)}\models\varphi
.
In this case it is easier to start with
w\models\delta(R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}w\models\delta(R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a&\Leftrightarrow&w\models\delta(R\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models(\delta(\mathit{One}(R)\land\mathbf{X}R^{\omega})\lor\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models(\delta(\mathit{One}(R))\land R^{\omega}\lor\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a\\
&\Leftrightarrow&w\models((\mathit{One}(R)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,R^{\omega})\lor\partial(R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}){\downarrow}a\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma}~\ref{lma:TT}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&a\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\models R^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w\models\partial(R){\downarrow}a\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&a\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\models R^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\exists u,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models\mathbf{X}R^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&a\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\models R^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\exists u\neq\epsilon,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}u\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models R^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\exists u=\epsilon,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}u\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models R^{\omega}\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}\\
&&\exists u\neq\epsilon,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}u\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models R^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\exists u,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}u\in\mathcal{L}(\partial(R){\downarrow}a)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\models R^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\exists u,v:w=uv\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}au\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}v\in(\mathcal{L}(R)\setminus\{\epsilon\})^{\omega}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&aw\in(\mathcal{L}(R)\setminus\{\epsilon\}){\cdot}(\mathcal{L}(R)\setminus\{\epsilon\})^{\omega}\Leftrightarrow aw\in(\mathcal{L}(R)\setminus\{\epsilon\})^{\omega}\Leftrightarrow aw\models R^{\omega}\end{array}

The other cases follow by de Morgan’s laws and laws of duality in
RLTL
.
The theorem follows by the induction principle.
∎

7.3.
\omega
-Regularity Modulo Theories

Here we lift the classical concept of
\omega
-regular
languages
(
Büchi 1960
;
McNaughton 1966
)
as the languages accepted by
ABW
so as to
be modulo
\mathcal{A}
, and show that
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
captures
\omega
-regularity
modulo
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that
L\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-regular

if
L=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})
for some
\mathcal{M}
that is an
\textit{NBW}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We lift the
following result from
(
Büchi 1960
;
McNaughton 1966
)
(see also

(
Thomas 1990
, 1.1. Theorem)
)
as to be modulo
\mathcal{A}
.
Recall that
\mathscr{L}(R^{\omega})=(\mathcal{L}(R){\setminus}\{\epsilon\})^{\omega}
.

Theorem 5 (
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-regularity).

A language
L\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-regular
\Leftrightarrow
there exist

\{R_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{S_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
in
\mathcal{R}^{\textsc{std}}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that

L=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}(R_{i}){\cdot}\mathscr{L}(S_{i}^{\omega})
.

Proof.

By using Lemma
2
and the Theorem in
(
McNaughton 1966
)
.
∎

Next we show that
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
captures
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-regularity.
We use the following
stepping
lemma of the transition function
of any
ABW
, the proof of which is based on the definition of accepting runs.
The lifting of
\hat{\varrho}(u,\phi)
below for
\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)

lets
\varrho(\varphi\diamond\psi){\downarrow}a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\varrho(\varphi){\downarrow}a\diamond\varrho(\psi){\downarrow}a
.

Lemma 7.

\forall\mathcal{M}\,{\in}\,\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}},\phi\,{\in}\,\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q_{\mathcal{M}}),u\,{\in}\,\mathbb{D}^{*},w\,{\in}\,\mathbb{D}^{\omega}:uw\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\Leftrightarrow w\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}_{\mathcal{M}}(u,\phi))
.

Recall the construction of the
\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}

\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
in
Definition
7.3
.
Theorem
6
(1)‘
\Rightarrow
’ is proved by using
Theorem
5
. Theorem
6
(2) (from
which also (1)‘
\Leftarrow
’ follows) is proved by extending the
inductive proof in
(
Vardi 1995
)
to handle suffix implications
and also makes use of Lemma
7
,
while closure formulas are additional base cases that are essentially
based on derivatives of
\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 6 (
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-Regularity of
\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
).

(1)

L\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
is
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
-regular
\Leftrightarrow

\exists\phi\in\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}:L=\mathscr{L}(\phi)

(2)

\forall\phi\in\textit{RLTL}_{\mathcal{A}}^{+}:\mathscr{L}(\phi)=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{\phi})

Although derivatives of
R^{\omega}
work correctly in
Theorem
4
, and therefore also when considering

\lnot R^{\omega}
because the semantics is based on
RLTL
,

the complemented derivatives arising from

\lnot\delta(R^{\omega})
would in general have incorrect
semantics as transitions of an
ABW
.

Moreover, sometimes
\omega
-closure
R^{\omega}
can be
avoided through
anchoring
: Let
\$
be a new predicate called
an
anchor
, not present in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
, in the extended algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with

\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}=\mathbb{D}\cup\{\texttt{\scriptsize\char 36\relax}\}
12
12

12

If

\mathcal{A}
is an SMT solver then
\$
can be a fresh uninterpreted
proposition, in which case
\$\mapsto\mathbf{false}
when considering
any predicate
\alpha\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
\$\mapsto\mathbf{true}
otherwise.

such
that
\llbracket\$\rrbracket_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}=\{\texttt{\scriptsize\char 36\relax}\}
and thus

\llbracket{\mathrm{!}}\$\rrbracket_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}=\mathbb{D}
.

&&\end{array}

Then

q_{1}=\lnot(\alpha\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}\phi)\equiv\alpha\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\lnot\mathbf{X}\phi
would be
considered as an
accepting
state and thus
incorrectly
accept

a^{\omega}
because
a^{\omega}\models(\alpha{\cdot}\alpha)^{\omega}
. On the other
hand, for
\boldsymbol{!\{}(\alpha{\cdot}\alpha){\ast}{\cdot}\$\boldsymbol{\}}

we get the transitions:
