\end{array}\end{math}
}
}\\
q_{1}=\mathbf{G}{\mathrm{!}}\$\land s_{1}&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}{\mathrm{!}}\$\land s_{0}\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,({\mathrm{!}}\$\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}{\mathrm{!}}\$))&&\\
q_{2}=\mathbf{G}{\mathrm{!}}\$&({\mathrm{!}}\$\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\mathbf{G}{\mathrm{!}}\$)&&\end{array}

where
{\mathrm{!}}\$
is obsolete in
\mathcal{A}
(becomes
\top
).
In particular observe that
a^{\omega}\notin\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{\lnot((\alpha\alpha)^{\omega})})
.

\Box

8.
Related Work

Derivatives

Transition regexes for extended regular
expressions
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
, a symbolic generalization of

Brzozowski derivatives

(
Brzozowski 1964
)
, is one of the inspirations
behind our work here. We view transition terms for LTL
as a symbolic generalization of
Vardi’s derivatives for LTL

(
Vardi 1995
)
.
Another source of inspiration in this context was
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2021
)

to lift the theory of automata modulo theories to
\omega
-languages.
LTL based
monitoring
also uses derivatives
(
Sen et al. 2003
;
Havelund and Roşu 2001
)
,
although not in the generalized form of symbolic derivatives modulo

\mathcal{A}
, but for a finite set of atomic propositions.
The relationship between the tableaux method for
LTL
(
Wolper 1985
)
and applying the concept of
linear factors from
(
Antimirov 1996
)
to LTL were
studied in
(
Sulzmann and Thiemann 2018
)
, implying a fundamental connection
between the two constructions.

Alternation elimination

Alternation elimination in regexes in
(
Stanford et al. 2021
)
through
symbolic regex derivatives amounts to incremental unfolding into NFAs
modulo
\mathcal{A}
(
\mathcal{A}
here is a
Unicode character theory
) – as a
symbolic unfolding of
Antimirov derivatives

(
Antimirov 1996
)
.
The work in
(
Antimirov and Mosses 1995
)
uses Horn-equational reasoning
as an alternative to derivatives for reasoning about regular expressions that use
intersection and also allows infinite alphabets.
Symbolic regex derivatives are used in the core of the regex decision
procedure in Z3
(
de Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
. The alternation elimination
algorithm
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
is further studied in-depth
(
Boker et al. 2010
)

with an improved lower bound for
ordered
ABW
s
. An open question related
to
(
Boker et al. 2010
, Theorem 4)
, proving state bound
2^{|\mathbb{D}|+n}
, is
its variant in the symbolic case when
\mathbb{D}
can be
infinite
.

Non-emptiness of
ABW
resulting from LTL is studied by Bloem, Ravi, and
Somenzi through classification into
weak
and

terminal
cases where the problem is easier than in the general case
(
Bloem et al. 1999
)
.

Fritz presents an LTL to
NBW
procedure
(
Fritz 2003
)
based on
simulation relations for
ABW
s, while starting with an
ABW
(based on
the Vardi construction) the procedure then computes simulation
relations on-the-fly, using delayed simulations and

\epsilon
-transitions. In the final phase the
ABW
is translated into
an
NBW
via
(
Miyano and Hayashi 1984
)
.

The Gerth et.al. algorithm
(
Gerth et al. 1995
)
uses
tableau
to
translate LTL into
NBW
. The construction first constructs a

Generalized Büchi automaton
(GBA) in tableau using the expansion
rules of Vardi derivatives, that is subsequently transformed, via
a well-known automata-theoretic encoding into
NBW
that preserves that

all acceptance conditions
of the GBA are visited infinitely
often. Tableau based techniques for LTL were initially studied by
Wolper
(
Wolper 1981
;
Wolper 1983
;
Wolper 1985
)
. A further extension of
the tableau based technique for LTL is introduced in
(
Couvreur 1999
)

using on-the-fly expansion of
transition Büchi automata
. The
key technique there is also rooted in what we call here Vardi
derivatives, that are called
fundamental identities
of Boolean
variables in that context, that reflect how the variables for the
subformulas are created inductively.
In regard to
tableau modulo
\mathcal{A}
in general,
it is an open and active research area, part of a general
effort to combine first-order deduction with modulo theories
with many open challenges
(
Burel et al. 2020
)
.

Vardi
(
Vardi 1994
, Theorem 14, Proof)
is the first LTL to

ABW
construction defined in terms of a
step-wise unwinding essentially as derivatives. This
construction is not symbolic, as it uses the next element to directly
compute a Boolean combination of successor states.

(
Tsay and Vardi 2021
)
give a full construction of LTL to

symbolic alternating co-Büchi automata.
While aspects of the
construction are similar to the one in our work, by being based on a
symbolic representation of
\rho(q,a)
– a key difference is in the
representation of
\varrho(q)
as a transition term providing a natural
separation of concerns between evaluation of transitions from their
target state formulas, that moreover works with
any
effective Boolean algebra
\mathcal{A}
.

Gastin and Oddoux
(
Gastin and Oddoux 2001
)
modify Vardi’s original
construction to produce very weak alternating co-Büchi automata
instead, that then are transformed into GBAs.
They take a step towards a symbolic representation by representing
transitions as relations, although treatment of the alphabet is still
non-symbolic. This representation allows them to develop
simplifications based on relational reasoning for eliminating implied
transitions and equivalent states
on-the-fly
. In the context of
our work, since these simplifications operate directly on the
representation of the transition relation, the direct correspondence
between formulas and states is lost.

Wulf et al.
(
Wulf et al. 2008
)
define
symbolic alternating Büchi
automata
(sABW) where transition relations are Boolean combinations
of literals and successor states. They further develop incremental
satisfiability and model checking methods using BDDs
(
Bryant 1986
)
both as the
alphabet theory and to represent sets of states. They do not give the
construction from LTL to sABW, but instead refer to the presentations
in
(
Vardi 1995
;
Gastin and Oddoux 2001
)
.

Muller, Saoudi and Schupp
(
Muller et al. 1988
)
were the first to state
and prove the theorem that LTL can be translated to Büchi
automata. The proof, however, does not use an inductive unwinding of
LTL formula but composes automata from subformulas instead and is very
different from derivatives – the construction uses weak alternating
automata over trees and a reduction
(
Muller et al. 1986
)
to Büchi
automata.

The ITE aspect of transition terms is not preserved in the above works
and the view of states as formulas, even if maintainable in some form
in a GBA, gets then lost in the translation to
NBW
.

Extensions of LTL with Background Theories

LTL with constraints

(
Demri and D’Souza 2007
)
is a fragment of first-order
LTL, where LTL with
Presburger arithmetic

(
Demri 2006
)
is a
typical extension of LTL. While many decidable fragments exist, such
as CLTL
(\mathcal{D})

(
Demri and D’Souza 2007
)
, constraint extensions in
general lead to undecidability. Further extensions of LTL over
infinite domains are introduced in
(
Grumberg et al. 2012
)
and
subsequently formalized with
generalized register automata

in
(
Grumberg et al. 2013
)
. In
(
Faran and Kupferman 2018
)
,

LTL with arithmetic or LTLA

is studied further with a focus on its decidable
and undecidable fragments, where
the model-checking problem of
the existential fragment is shown to be in PSPACE and
the problem is studied also for hierarchical systems.

The synthesis problem of

nondeterministic looping word automata with arithmetic

is studied in
(
Faran and Kupferman 2020
)

by using games, where the languages are
also infinite words over an infinite alphabet,
in this case over rationals paired with a finite alphabet.

Recent work in
(
Geatti et al. 2022
)
extends LTL with modulo theories over

finite
strings, the theory is also generally

undecidable
.

In the above works derivatives are not being used and the extensions
are in general orthogonal to what we propose here as
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
that maintains
decidability and algorithms modulo arbitrary effective Boolean algebra
\mathcal{A}
,
with an SMT solver as a prime example of
a general purpose effective Boolean algebra.
In some aspect
\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}

bridges a gap between classical LTL and certain decidable fragments of
theory specific extensions of LTL.

PSL and SPOT

PSL
(
Eisner and Fisman 2006
)
is the
IEEE 1850 Property Specification
Language
. Its primary purpose is for formal specification of
concurrent systems. SPOT
(
Duret-Lutz 2022
)
is a state-of-the-art verification tool that
supports a large subset of PSL.
RLTL
covers a core subset of
PSL that is consistent with the semantics of suffix implications in
SPOT, but
differs
in one important aspect in regard to
weak
closure
(and its negation), namely that, for any nullable regex
R
,

\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\top
. For a PSL formula
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
the corresponding
weak closure in
RLTL
is
\boldsymbol{\{}R\land\top{+}\boldsymbol{\}}
. This
difference is driven by the semantics of derivatives that
effectively
enforces
the law
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\top

whenever
R
is nullable, in order to to maintain an algebraically
well-behaved system of rules. This also means that some basic rewrite
rules in SPOT (such as
\boldsymbol{\{}r{\ast}\boldsymbol{\}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}
) are
invalid

for formulas in
RLTL
.

RLTL
also supports regex
complement
that comes naturally
because of the built-in duality law of transition regexes:

\texttt{\char 126\relax}(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,g)=(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\texttt{\char 126\relax}f\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\texttt{\char 126\relax}g)

(
Stanford et al. 2021
, Lemma 4.2)
. It is
difficult and highly impractical to support
˜
by other means,
because it would in general require
determinization
of symbolic
finite automata that is avoided by the duality law that propagates

˜

lazily
.

A key difference is that
RLTL
lifts (a core
subset of) PSL as to be modulo theories: while in PSL the atomic regexes

\alpha
are Boolean combinations of
propositions
, in
RLTL

\alpha
can be any SMT formula.

Finally, further operators can be
supported by simply defining their
symbolic derivatives. E.g., for regular
expression
fusion

(R\,{\oplus}\,S)
,

\mathcal{L}(R\,{\oplus}\,S)=\{xay\mid x,y\in\mathbb{D}^{*},a\in\mathbb{D},xa\in\mathcal{L}(R),ay\in\mathcal{L}(S)\}
,

\partial(R\,{\oplus}\,S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}(\mathit{One}(R)\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,\partial(S))\lor(\partial(R)\,{\oplus}\,S)\qquad\mathit{Null}(R\,{\oplus}\,S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textsc{\tiny def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{false}

where again (
2
) lifts
\lambda r.r\,{\oplus}\,S
to transition regexes (extended with fusion).
Additional rewrite rules, such as
(\hskip-1.19995pt)\oplus S\cong\bot
, are also added.
Then, for example, for
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and
R=\alpha{\ast}\oplus\beta{\ast}
,
we get the following derivative, that we compare side-by-side with
the derivative of
S=\alpha{\ast}{\cdot}(\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\beta){\cdot}\beta{\ast}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\partial(R)&\cong&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\beta{\ast}\lor R)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,R)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\\
\partial(S)&\cong&(\alpha\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\beta\,{\boldsymbol{{?}}}\,(\beta{\ast}\lor S)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,S)\,{\boldsymbol{{:}}}\,\bot)\end{array}

where
R
and
S
are clearly equivalent regexes, which is also reflected in their derivatives.

9.
Future Work

Optimizations

There are several layers of optimizations that can be
applied to the apporach presented here.
All leaves in INF can be maintained as

antichains
based on the property of
maintaining all satisfiers in a minimal form – recall that (
3
)
is the antichain of
\mathrm{DNF}(\phi)
with respect to
\subseteq
.
Use of antichains is a powerful general technique
also used in classical LTL
(
Wulf et al. 2008
;
Filiot et al. 2009
)
.

The construction of
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
can be incremental just as in the
case of
\mathcal{M}_{R}
and can moreover eliminate dead states on-the-fly,
e.g., by using the algorithm in
(
Stanford and Veanes 2023
)
.
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
can also
be based on an
NFA
of
R
or symbolic generalization
of
partial derivatives

(
Antimirov 1996
)
. NFAs require only minor
modifications for support in
RLTL
but can be more economical at
least in the case of
\mathcal{R}^{\textsc{std}}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}}
without
\land
and

˜
). Use of NFAs avoids lazy
determinization
that happens
implicitly in Lemma
4
that is based on a symbolic
generalization of derivatives in
(
Brzozowski 1964
)
. DFAs can be beneficial
when working with

negated weak closures
to avoid conjunctions
(and alternation elimination) that would otherwise arise
upon negation of transition terms.

For
\mathcal{M}_{\lnot R^{\omega}}
general
NBW
complementation of
\mathcal{M}_{R^{\omega}}

can be supported by first lifting the result that
NBW
s are closed
under complement
(
Büchi 1960
)
(see
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 4)
) as to
be modulo
\mathcal{A}
, through mintermization, and then develop symbolic
algorithms for complementing
\mathcal{M}_{R^{\omega}}
modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

At the
transition
level: ITEs can potentially be
represented more efficiently by
generic BDDs

(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2017b
)
, with
leaves as terminals, ordering the predicates in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
and maintaining
that order internally in Boolean operations,
or by utilizing
Shannon expansions

(
Shannon 1949
)
in general when
working with ITEs.

At the
automata
level:
simulation

algorithms
(
Gurumurthy et al. 2002
;
Fritz and Wilke 2002
;
Fritz and Wilke 2005
;
Fritz 2003
;
Etessami et al. 2005
;
Mayr and Clemente 2013
)

have been studied to reduce the state space, that have moreover been
improved with
antichains

(
Abdulla et al. 2010
)
and
bisimulations
up to congruence

(
Bonchi and Pous 2013
)
. Some of these algorithms have also
been lifted to
modulo
\mathcal{A}
in the case of
deterministic
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2014
;
D’Antoni and Veanes 2016
)
and
nondeterministic
(
D’Antoni and Veanes 2017a
;
Holík et al. 2018
)
finite automata, where the
underlying technique is to work with
symmetric differences of
predicates in
\mathcal{A}
to avoid explicit iteration over the alphabet
as
in the classical Hopcroft DFA minimization
(
Hopcroft 1971
)
and
Paige-Tarjan relational coarsest partition
(
Paige and Tarjan 1987
)
algorithms –
both being essentially
bisimulation
algorithms for DFAs and
NFAs, respectively. It is therefore plausible that an analogous
treatment of predicates in
\mathcal{A}
can be extended for simulation,
bisimulation, and further
NBW
algorithms studied in
(
Kuperberg et al. 2021
)

when lifted to modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

The deterministic case

Symbolic derivatives might give new insights for
DBW

algorithms
(
Kurshan 1987
;
Finkbeiner and Schewe 2005
;
Baier and Katoen 2008
;
Babiak et al. 2012
;
Baarir and Duret-Lutz 2014
;
Kupferman and Rosenberg 2010
;
Tourneur and Duret-Lutz 2017
;
Diekert et al. 2015
)

when lifted to modulo
\mathcal{A}
.

Other extensions

Symbolic derivatives can be developed
with laws that provide incremental/lazy unfolding of
counters
, that
may be applicable to
LTL with
counting

(
Laroussinie et al. 2010
)
.
The
past-time
operator does not increase expressivity of
LTL
(
Gabbay et al. 1980
)
– it is nevertheless practically very
useful
(
Lichtenstein et al. 1985
;
Laroussinie and Schnoebelen 2002
;
Kupferman and Pnueli 1995
;
Kupferman et al. 2012
)
. Symbolic derivatives
are inherently “forward looking”,
so understanding how to efficiently handle past-time with them is intriguing.

Finally, a larger open problem is if the derivative based view can also be
developed modulo
\mathcal{A}
for
Computation Tree Logic
(CTL) or even
CTL* (a hybrid of LTL and CTL)
(
Piterman and Pnueli 2018
)
.
Infinite trees
rather than
words are the core elements in the semantics in this case.
Derivatives for finite trees have recently been studied in
(
Attou et al. 2021
)
.

10.
Conclusion

We have shown how symbolic transition terms and derivatives can be used to
define a realizable symbolic semantics for
(alternating) Büchi automata and linear temporal logic (LTL).
The semantics is parameterized by an effective Boolean algebra
for the base alphabetic domain, which enables it to apply to

\omega
-languages and infinite alphabets in an algebraically
well-defined and precise manner. This framework allows syntactic
rewrite rules for LTL extended with regular expressions (RLTL)
to be applied
on-the-fly
during alternation
elimination, where they simultaneously respect the semantics of RLTL
formulas and their alternating Büchi automata. Similarly to the new
alternation elimination algorithm, we believe there is a rich
landscape of further optimizations and
algorithms yet to be discovered.

Appendix A
Proofs

Proof of Corollary
1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}_{1}\land\mathcal{N}_{2}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
as
the
ABW
in the algorithm and let
\mathcal{N}=\textrm{\AE}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)
.
We use that all leaves of
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
are disjunctions of states, i.e.,
in INF, all leaves are sets whose members are singleton sets.
Initially
S_{0}\leftarrow\mathrm{DNF}(Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!1\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}\land Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!2\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}})\otimes\{\emptyset\}
.
Therefore
S_{0}
consists of states of the form

\{\{q^{0}_{1},q^{0}_{2}\}\}\otimes\{\emptyset\}=\langle\{q^{0}_{1},q^{0}_{2}\}\setminus F,\{q^{0}_{1},q^{0}_{2}\}\cap F\rangle
,
where
q^{0}_{1}\in Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!1\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}
and
q^{0}_{2}\in Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\!2\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}}
.
The invariant
U\cap F=\emptyset
holds.
We now consider
q=\langle U,V\rangle
in the algorithm where
f
is computed and
show that all leaves of
f
are sets of states of one of the four cases with

q_{1}\in Q_{1}
and
q_{2}\in Q_{2}
:

1)\,q{=}\langle\{q_{1},q_{2}\},\emptyset\rangle\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}2)\,q{=}\langle\emptyset,\{q_{1},q_{2}\}\rangle\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}3)\,q{=}\langle\{q_{1}\},\{q_{2}\}\rangle\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}4)\,q{=}\langle\{q_{2}\},\{q_{1}\}\rangle.

Cases 1 and 2
:

f=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(\{q_{1},q_{2}\})\otimes\{\emptyset\}=g\otimes\{\emptyset\}
where

g=\mathit{INF}(\varrho_{1}(q_{1})\land\varrho_{2}(q_{2}))

and so in
g
each leaf
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a set of sets
of the form
X=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
for some
p_{1}\in Q_{1}
and
p_{2}\in Q_{2}
.
Then
\langle X\setminus F,\emptyset\cup(X\cap F)\rangle
clearly corresonds to one of the four cases.

Cases 3 and 4
: Let
\{i,j\}=\{1,2\}
. Then

f=\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{i})\otimes\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q_{j})=\mathit{INF}(\varrho_{i}(q_{i}))\otimes\mathit{INF}(\varrho_{j}(q_{j}))

where each member
X
of a leaf in
\mathit{INF}(\varrho_{i}(q_{i}))
is a singleton
\{p_{i}\}
with
p_{i}\in Q_{i}
,
and each member
Y
of a leaf in
\mathit{INF}(\varrho_{j}(q_{j}))
is a singleton
\{p_{j}\}
with
p_{j}\in Q_{j}
.
It follows that
\langle X{\setminus}F,Y\cup(X\cap F)\rangle
belongs to one of the cases 2, 3 or 4,
because if
p_{i}\in F_{i}
then
X{\setminus}F=\emptyset
and
X\cap F=X
else

X{\setminus}F=X
and
X\cap F=\emptyset
.

In each case the invariant
(X{\setminus}F)\cap F=\emptyset
is clearly maintained.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2
.

Let
I
be a
finite subset of
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}
. In the following, it is convenient to
identify each minterm
\alpha\in\textit{Minterms}(I)
by the unique subset

I_{\alpha}
of
I
such that
\alpha\equiv{{\textrm{\Large\&}_{\gamma\in I_{\alpha}}}\gamma\mathrel{\&}{\textrm{\Large\&}_{\gamma\in I\setminus I_{\alpha}}}{\mathrm{!}}\gamma}
.
Let
\textit{Conds}(\phi)
denote the set of all predicates in
\Psi_{\!\mathcal{A}}

that occur in
\phi\in\textit{LTL}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

We first translate
\phi
into classical LTL as follows.
Let
I=\textit{Conds}(\phi)
,
and for each
\iota\in I
let
p_{\iota}
be a fresh proposition in

P=\{p_{\iota}\}_{\iota\in I}
. Let
\Sigma=\textit{Minterms}(I)
. Map each
minterm
\alpha\in\Sigma
uniquely to
\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor=\{p_{\iota}\in P\mid\iota\in I_{\alpha}\}
.
Thus,
p_{\iota}\in\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor
iff
\iota
is a positive literal of
\alpha
.

Let
\pi_{\alpha}
denote the conjunction

\bigwedge_{p\in\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}p\land\bigwedge_{p\in P\setminus\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\lnot p
that encodes the minterm
\alpha

propositionally.
Now, for all

\iota\in I
, let

{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}=\bigvee_{\alpha\in\Sigma,\alpha\Rightarrow\iota}\pi_{\alpha}

denote the propositional encoding of
\iota
,
where
\alpha\Rightarrow\iota
means that
\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\iota\rrbracket

that is equivalent to
\alpha{\mathrel{\&}}\iota\nequiv\bot
by definition of minterms.
Observe that

{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}=\bot
precisely when
\iota
is
unsatisfiable
in
\mathcal{A}
.

For clarity let
\models_{P}
stand for the classical semantics of LTL over
P
,
while
\models
is defined in (
8
–
14
).
As earlier,
for all
a\in\mathbb{D}
let
\widehat{a}\in\Sigma
denote the minterm such that
a\in\llbracket\widehat{a}\rrbracket
.
It follows that

\lfloor{\widehat{a}}\rfloor\models_{P}{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}\;\Leftrightarrow\;(\widehat{a}\Rightarrow\iota)\;\Leftrightarrow\;a\in\llbracket\iota\rrbracket\;\Leftrightarrow\;a\models\iota

For
a\in\mathbb{D}
and
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
,
lift
\widehat{a}
to
\widehat{w}\in\Sigma^{\omega}

and lift

\lfloor{\widehat{\alpha}}\rfloor
to
\lfloor{\widehat{w}}\rfloor\in(2^{P})^{\omega}
.
Finally, let
{\,\widetilde{\phi}\,}
be the conversion of
\phi
to classical LTL
by replacing each predicate
\iota\in I
in
\phi
by
{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}
.
Then

w\models\phi\;\Leftrightarrow\;\lfloor{\widehat{w}}\rfloor\models_{P}{\,\widetilde{\phi}\,}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{V95}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\;\lfloor{\widehat{w}}\rfloor\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{{\,\widetilde{\phi}\,}})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\;\widehat{w}\in\mathscr{L}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}})\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:mt}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\;w\in\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{\phi})

where V95 is
(
Kupferman 2018
, Theorem 24)
;
(\star)
follows on one
hand from renaming of the alphabet symbols, each symbol

\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor\in 2^{P}
is uniformly renamed to the symbol

\alpha\in\Sigma
; on the other hand,
\widehat{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}}
introduces

\Sigma
through mintermization of
I
where the semantics is
preserved modulo renaming by definition of
{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}

that precisely encodes

|_{\alpha\in\Sigma,\alpha\Rightarrow\iota}\alpha

that is the mintermized equivalent form of
\iota
in
\mathcal{A}
as
the disjunction of all the minterms contained in
\iota
.
∎

The construction of
{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}
above can also use
bit-blasting

where minterms are directly encoded as
independent
bits by propositions

\{p_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Sigma}
. This alternative encoding
may be of practical interest is an actual conversion to the classical setting.

The following example illustrates the constructions used in the proof
of Theorem
2
.

Example A.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Boolean algebra of predicates over integers
and
\phi=(x{<}1)\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}(0{<}x)
with
x
of type
integer
.
Then
I=\{x{<}1,0{<}x\}
,
P=\{p_{x{<}1},p_{0{<}x}\}

and

\Sigma=\{x{<}1{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}0{<}x,0{<}x{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}x{<}1\}
.
The formulas
{\,\widetilde{\iota}\,}
for
\iota\in I
are

{\,\widetilde{x{<}1}\,}=p_{x{<}1}{\land}\lnot p_{0{<}x}
and

{\,\widetilde{0{<}x}\,}=p_{0{<}x}{\land}\lnot p_{x{<}1}
.
And
thus
{\,\widetilde{\phi}\,}=(p_{x{<}1}{\land}\lnot p_{0{<}x})\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}(p_{0{<}x}{\land}\lnot p_{x{<}1})
.

If we instead consider
x
as a
real
then

\Sigma
also includes
0{<}x{\mathrel{\&}}x{<}1
while

{\mathrm{!}}0{<}x{\mathrel{\&}}{\mathrm{!}}x{<}1
is still unsatisfiable.
Then

{\,\widetilde{x{<}1}\,}=p_{x{<}1}{\land}\lnot p_{0{<}x}\lor p_{x{<}1}{\land}p_{0{<}x}
that simplifies
to
{\,\widetilde{x{<}1}\,}=p_{x{<}1}
, and similarly we get that
{\,\widetilde{0{<}x}\,}=p_{0{<}x}
,
in which case
{\,\widetilde{\phi}\,}=p_{x{<}1}\mathrel{\mathbf{R}}p_{0{<}x}
.

\Box

Proof of Lemma
6
.

We show (1), the proof of (2) is analogous.

Direction
(\Rightarrow)
: Let
w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi
.
Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that (
13
) holds.
If
j=0
then
w_{(0..)}=w\models\psi
and we are done.
If
j>0
then, for all
i<j
,
w_{(i..)}\models\varphi
and

w_{(j..)}=(w_{(1..)})_{(j-1..)}\models\psi
. In particular,
w_{(0..)}=w\models\varphi
and
there exists
k=j-1\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(w_{(1..)})_{(k..)}\models\psi
and
for all
i<k
,
(w_{(1..)})_{(i..)}\models\varphi
. So, by (
13
),

w_{(1..)}\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi
.

Direction
(\Leftarrow)
: If
w\models\psi
then
w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi

follows immediately from (
13
).
If
w\models\varphi
and
w_{(1..)}\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi
then
there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that, by (
13
),

(w_{(1..)})_{(k..)}\models\psi
and
for all
i<k
,
(w_{(1..)})_{(i..)}\models\varphi
.
It follows that for
j=k+1
,
w_{(j..)}\models\psi

and
for all
i<j
,
w_{(i..)}\models\varphi
.
So
w\models\varphi\mathrel{\mathbf{U}}\psi
by (
13
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

Fix
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{A},Q,Q^{\textrm{\tiny 0}}_{\textrm{\scriptsize$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\\[-17.20844pt]
\\[-8.19447pt]
\end{array}$}},\varrho,F)
and
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
.
We prove the following statement by induction over the length of
u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
:

\forall\phi\,{\in}\,\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q):uw\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\Leftrightarrow w\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}(u,\phi))

The empty word case holds by definition because
\hat{\varrho}(\epsilon,\phi)=\phi
.
For the nonempty word case we first prove the following statement:

(34)

\forall q\in Q,av\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}:av\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)\Leftrightarrow v\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)

Direction
\Rightarrow
of (
34
)
:
Let
av\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)
. So there exists an accepting
run
\tau
for
av
from
q
.
This implies that that there exists

X\in\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q){\downarrow}a
such that, for each
p\in X
,

\tau
has has an immediate subtree
\tau_{p}
accepting a run for

v
from
p
. In other words,

v\in\bigcap_{p\in X}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(p)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\bigwedge X)\subseteq\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q){\downarrow}a)=\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)

Direction
\Leftarrow
of (
34
)
: Let
v\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\varrho(q){\downarrow}a)
.
So
v\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q){\downarrow}a)
and therefore
there exists
X\in\boldsymbol{\varrho}(q){\downarrow}a
such that

v\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\bigwedge X)
. So there exist accepting
runs
\tau_{p}
for
v
from
p
for each
p\in X
.
Let
\tau
be the infinite tree with root labelled by
q

having
\tau_{p}
, for
p\in X
, as its immediate subtrees.
Then
\tau
is an accepting run for
av
from
q
and thus
av\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(q)
.

Next we lift (
34
) to
\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q)
where
\varrho
is lifted similarly (as with derivatives) and
use Lemma
1
.

(35)

\forall\phi\in\mathbb{B}^{+}\!(Q),av\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}:av\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\Leftrightarrow v\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\varrho(\phi){\downarrow}a)

We now complete the induction case of the main statement.

auw\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:tf2})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}uw\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\varrho(\phi){\downarrow}a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}(u,\varrho(\phi){\downarrow}a))\Leftrightarrow w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}(au,\phi))

The lemma follows by the induction principle.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6
.

We first prove (1)‘
\Rightarrow
’,
and then (2) which imples (1)‘
\Leftarrow
’.

Proof of ‘
\Rightarrow
’ of (1)

Let
L=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M})
for some
\mathcal{M}\in\textit{ABW}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
let
R_{i},S_{i}\in\mathcal{R}^{\textsc{std}}_{\mathcal{A}}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
be given by Theorem
5
.
Let

\begin{array}[]{l}\phi=\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}\;\textrm{where}\;\phi_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}S_{i}^{\omega}\lor(R_{i}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}S_{i}^{\omega}),&\textrm{{if} $\mathit{Null}(R_{i})$;}\\
R_{i}\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\mathbf{X}S_{i}^{\omega},&\textrm{{otherwise}.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Fix
\phi_{i}
and assume that
R_{i}
is nullable. Then

w\models\phi_{i}
iff
w\models S_{i}^{\omega}

or there exists
u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}
and
v\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
such that
w=uv
and

uv_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(R_{i})
and
v\models\mathbf{X}S_{i}^{\omega}

iff there is
u\in\mathbb{D}^{+}
and
v\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
such that

u\in\mathcal{L}(R_{i})
and
v\models S_{i}^{\omega}
.
So
w\models\phi_{i}
iff

w\in\mathcal{L}(R_{i}){\cdot}\mathscr{L}(S_{i}^{\omega})
.
The case when
R_{i}
is not nullable is analogous.
It follows that
L=\mathscr{L}(\phi)
.

Proof of (2)

The proof is by extending the
original proof in
(
Vardi 1995
)
with the additional constructs.
We do not repeat the proof of the
LTL
fragment for the
modulo
\mathcal{A}
case because it would repeat very similar arguments.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
.

In the case of
\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
we get the
DBW

\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
by fixpoint construction of derivatives
whose
accepting
states are all the states
\boldsymbol{\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}
such that
r
is alive.
In the case of
R^{\omega}
we get an
NBW

\mathcal{M}_{R^{\omega}}
similarly but whose
only accepting

state is
R^{\omega}
.
For
negated weak closure

\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}
the construction of
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}

is analogous with
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}
except that in
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{!\{}R\boldsymbol{\}}}

a state
\boldsymbol{!\{}r\boldsymbol{\}}
is accepting iff
r
is dead.
It follows in all those cases that
\mathscr{L}(\phi)=\mathscr{L}(\mathcal{M}_{\phi})
.

The main cases are
\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}
and
\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}
because they involve induction over

\textit{RLTL}^{+}
formulas by building on the proof in
(
Vardi 1995
)
.
By construction of
\mathcal{M}
we have that
\delta(q)\cong\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}(q)
and we let
\varrho=\varrho_{\mathcal{M}}
below.
We also use that

\forall R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{A}},a\in\mathbb{D},u\in\mathbb{D}^{*}:a\in\llbracket\mathit{One}(\hat{\partial}(u,R))\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow ua\in\mathcal{L}(R)
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{D}^{\omega}
.
Let
w_{(..i)}

denote the finite prefix of
w
up to (including) the element
w_{i}
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(R{\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:eimpl-deriv}),\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w_{(1..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}((\varrho(\mathit{One}(R)\land\phi)\lor(\partial(R){\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)){\downarrow}w_{0})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:TT}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&(w_{0}{\in}\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(1..)}{\in}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}(w_{0},\phi)))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w_{(1..)}{\in}\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{0},R){\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&(w_{0}{\in}\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi))\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w_{(1..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{0},R){\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}})^{k}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\bigvee_{i<k}(w_{(..i)}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(i..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi))\\
&&\mathrel{\mathbf{or}}w_{(k..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{(..k{-}1)},R)\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{(e)}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\exists i\geq 0:w_{(..i)}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(i..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\exists i\geq 0:w_{(..i)}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(i..)}\models\phi\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:eimpl})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models R{\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi\end{array}

In
(\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}})^{k}
we repeat the previous unfolding a finite
number
k
times. In (e) we use the key property that all
states
r\mathrel{{\diamond}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi
in
\mathcal{M}
are
rejecting

and can therefore only be visited
finitely
often in any accepting
run for
w
. Thus,
the disjunct with
w_{(k..)}
acts as
\bot
for large enough
k
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}w\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(R{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:uimpl-deriv}),\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w_{(1..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}((\varrho(\mathit{One}(R)\rightarrow\phi)\land(\partial(R){\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)){\downarrow}w_{0})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:TT}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&({w_{0}\,{\in}\,\mathcal{L}(R)}\Rightarrow{w_{(1..)}\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\varrho}(w_{0},\phi))})\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(1..)}\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{0},R){\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&(w_{0}\,{\in}\,\mathcal{L}(R)\Rightarrow w\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi))\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(1..)}\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{0},R){\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}})^{m}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\bigwedge_{i<m}(w_{(..i)}\,{\in}\,\mathcal{L}(R)\Rightarrow w_{(i..)}\,{\in}\,\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi))\\
&&\mathrel{\mathbf{and}}w_{(m..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\hat{\partial}(w_{(..m{-}1)},R)\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\textrm{u})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\forall i\geq 0:w_{(..i)}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\Rightarrow w_{(i..)}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{IH}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&\forall i\geq 0:w_{(..i)}\in\mathcal{L}(R)\Rightarrow w_{(i..)}\models\phi\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:uimpl})}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}&w\models R{\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}}\phi\end{array}

In
(\textrm{Lma~\ref{lma:tf}})^{m}
we repeat the unfolding any finite
number
m
times. In (u) we use the key property that all
states
r\mathrel{\raisebox{0.59998pt}{\scriptsize$\square$}\hskip-1.30005pt{\shortrightarrow}}\phi
in
\mathcal{M}
are
accepting

and do not interfere with the first conjunct. The second
conjunct with
w_{(m..)}
acts as
\top
in the limit.

The statement (2) follows by the induction principle
and the theorem follows.
∎
